The experiment supports a narrower conclusion than a successful transfer certificate: the policy's response is not adequately supported on the audit grid, and low displacement does not demonstrate superior trading, welfare, or systemic protection. The reward design strongly penalizes inventory holding and turnover, creating an active avoidance mechanism rather than price stabilization.

\paragraph{Multiscale perturbation audit, reward sensitivity, and turnover matching.}
To verify that the out-of-class finding is not an artifact of wide-grid evaluation, a multiscale perturbation radius audit evaluates the response function across five nested radii $r \in \{0.05, 0.10, 0.25, 0.50, 1.00\}$ (Supplement Section~S14.6 and Table~S14.6). Across all radii and all ten policies, $R^2(r) \le 0.2449 \ll 0.70$. At the innermost radius ($r = 0.05$), all policies yield $R^2 = 0.0000$ due to complete dead-band action inertia induced by the penalties; at $r = 0.10$, local action variation appears with mean $R^2 = 0.0955$ and slope $\partial u/\partial x = 1.30$, but the response is a discrete step function whose fit decays to $R^2 = 0.0530$ at $r = 1.00$.

Furthermore, a reward-penalty sensitivity study (Supplement Section~S14.7 and Table~S14.7) evaluates policies under four alternative penalty configurations. Eliminating the inventory penalty ($\lambda_I = 0$) triples price dislocation from $0.0279$ to $0.0807$ and causes dealer capacity halts in $65.0\%$ of episodes, while leaving all policies outside the linear class ($R^2 \le 0.1498$). Finally, calibrating a static random trader to each policy's individual realized turnover (Supplement Section~S14.8 and Table~S14.8) matches each policy's frozen price dislocation to within $\$0.001$ across all ten policies (mean difference $-0.00068$, range $[-0.00096, -0.00019]$). This establishes that the low price dislocation of learned policies is an inventory-avoidance artifact rather than genuine stabilizing market-making.

\subsection{Strategic Subgame Perfect Equilibrium, Boundary Chipping, and Predatory Shorting}
\label{sec:strategic_game_theory}
The macroscopic stability theorems derived above assume that algorithmic counterparties follow parameterized linear or kernel policies. In real-world institutional deployments, however, sophisticated algorithmic participants may act strategically, actively gaming supervisory bounds or engaging in predatory trading against distressed counterparties \citep{brunnermeier2005predatory}. We formally analyze the subgame perfect Nash equilibrium (SPNE) of two canonical strategic attacks: boundary chipping and predatory shorting.

\paragraph{Strategic boundary chipping and cumulative inventory boundedness.}
Consider an unconstrained profit-seeking agent facing a supervisory per-step inventory or order rate constraint $r^* = B / \mathcal{K}(J)$ derived from Theorem~\ref{thm:m5_kreiss_solvency}. To evade single-step throttling thresholds, a strategic agent may deploy a \textit{boundary-chipping strategy}, repeatedly submitting maximal sub-threshold orders:
\begin{equation}
u_t = r^* - \epsilon, \qquad \epsilon > 0.
\end{equation}
Under naive stateless per-step filters, an agent could indefinitely accumulate inventory $\sum_{t=1}^T u_t \to \infty$, destabilizing the clearinghouse. Under the institutional AMIP protocol (\texttt{research\_extension/game\_theory/strategic\_equilibrium.py}), the supervisory guard maintains stateful cumulative inventory tracking $|I_t| = |\sum_{s=1}^t u_s| \le r^*$.

\begin{theorem}[Boundary Chipping Boundedness (Theorem G1)]
\label{thm:boundary_chipping_bounded}
Let a strategic agent play the boundary-chipping strategy $u_t = r^* - \epsilon$ for $\epsilon \in (0, r^*)$. Under stateful cumulative tracking, the agent's inventory accumulates monotonically until hitting the boundary in exactly
\begin{equation}
\tau = \left\lceil \frac{r^*}{r^* - \epsilon} \right\rceil
\end{equation}
steps. For all $t > \tau$, the remaining capacity is $\max(0, r^* - |I_t|) = 0$, forcing all subsequent orders to be strictly clamped to zero ($u_t = 0$). The total accumulated inventory is strictly bounded by $\sup_{t \ge 0} |I_t| \le r^*$, proving that boundary chipping cannot breach the Kreiss safe solvency radius.
\end{theorem}
Numerical verification across four perturbation scales $\epsilon \in \{0.01, 0.10, 1.00, 5.00\}$ with $r^* = 51.05$ confirms that the invariant $|I_t| \le r^*$ is maintained with $0.0\%$ leakage (\texttt{results/provenance/strategic\_equilibrium\_results.json}).

\paragraph{Predatory shorting invalidation via passive pegged liquidity.}
A second critical game-theoretic vulnerability arises during market distress. Following \citet{brunnermeier2005predatory}, suppose an institutional dealer faces distressed liquidation. A strategic predator can aggressively short sell into the market, driving the price down to trigger the dealer's capacity halt, subsequently buying back at depressed liquidation prices to lock in a predatory arbitrage.
Under conventional exchange circuit breakers that enforce an outright trading ban, the dealer withdraws all quotes. The predator dumps short orders into an empty book, depressing the price by $4.0\%$ and extracting $+\$100.00$ in predatory profit.
In sharp contrast, the AMIP RFC-001 specification mandates that throttled orders be converted into passive limit orders pegged at the touch ($25\%$ passive liquidity injection):
\begin{theorem}[Anti-Predatory Equilibrium under AMIP RFC-001 (Theorem G2)]
\label{thm:anti_predatory}
Under an outright trading halt, bid depth collapses to zero, rendering predatory shorting strictly profitable ($\Pi_{\text{pred}} = +\$100.00 > 0$). Under AMIP RFC-001, converting $25\%$ of distressed volume into passive touch-pegged limit orders provides structural bid-side depth. The predator's selling flow executes against this guaranteed bid liquidity, causing the price to stabilize (maximum price dislocation $0.0\%$, final mid-price $\$17{,}182.50$) while the predator absorbs adverse execution costs. Predatory profit drops to $\Pi_{\text{pred}} = -\$68.75 < 0$. Consequently, within this stylized single-venue pricing model, predatory shorting yields strictly negative expected value ($\Pi_{\text{pred}} < 0$), removing the incentive for predatory short runs in this subgame perfect equilibrium.
\end{theorem}

\paragraph{Empirical Counterparty Attribution via FIX 5.0 SP2 Drop-Copy and BBL Estimation.}
To bridge Theorem~G2 with institutional clearinghouse practice, we implement counterparty-attributed streaming surveillance under Contract~$I_1$ via standard FIX~5.0~SP2 Drop-Copy feeds (\texttt{research\_extension/game\_theory/structural\_inverse\_game.py}). In anonymous markets (Contract~$I_0$), the Swapped-Feedback Non-Identifiability Theorem (\cref{thm:swapped_feedback}) establishes that an aggressive predator and a distressed dealer cannot be distinguished from public trades alone. Under FIX Drop-Copy message parsing (Tag 11 \texttt{ClOrdID}, Tag 448 \texttt{PartyID}, Tag 38 \texttt{OrderQty}), order streams are partitioned by counterparty. Applying the two-step structural forward-simulation estimator of \citet{bajari2007estimating} recovers the true behavioral parameters: the distressed dealer is identified with inventory penalty $\hat{\lambda}_{\text{dealer}} = 0.0500$ ($R^2 = 0.9936$), while the unconstrained predator is identified with $\hat{\lambda}_{\text{pred}} = 0.0050$ ($R^2 = 0.9389$; \texttt{results/provenance/structural\_inverse\_game\_results.json}). This confirms that institutional counterparty attribution resolves anonymous non-identifiability, enabling selective intervention against predatory accounts without penalizing distressed market makers.

\subsection{Welfare Economics and the Stability--Price Discovery Pareto Frontier: Theorem W1}
\label{sec:welfare_pareto_frontier}
A fundamental trade-off in financial architecture exists between systemic solvency preservation and informational price discovery efficiency \citep{grossman1980impossibility,kyle1985continuous}. Complete market laissez-faire permits instantaneous price discovery but exposes clearinghouses and dealers to unbounded feedback cascades. Conversely, crude trading halts completely eliminate solvency deficits at the catastrophic cost of halting price discovery entirely.
We formalize this trade-off along the continuous policy frontier $\theta \in [0, 1]$, where $\theta$ governs the pre-trade throttling intensity under AMIP RFC-001 (\texttt{simulations/welfare\_pareto\_frontier.py}).

\begin{figure*}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.88\textwidth]{figures/fig_welfare_pareto_frontier.pdf}
\caption{The Stability--Price Discovery Pareto Frontier under AMIP RFC-001. Left axis (blue circles): price discovery delay (steps to $95\%$ convergence toward the fundamental price). Right axis (red squares): systemic dealer insolvency deficit in thousands of USD. Unprotected trading ($\theta = 0.0$) allows rapid discovery but causes capital depletion. Complete trading halts ($\theta = 1.0$) eliminate insolvency losses but inflict infinite discovery delay (pricing failure). The institutional AMIP operating point ($\theta = 0.25$, green highlight) eliminates $100\%$ of systemic insolvency deficits ($\$0.00$ loss) while introducing minimal price discovery delay ($20$ steps), achieving the global Pareto optimum.}
\label{fig:welfare_pareto_frontier}
\end{figure*}

\begin{theorem}[Stability--Discovery Pareto Optimum (Theorem W1)]
\label{thm:welfare_pareto}
Let the market undergo a fundamental valuation jump $\Delta p^* = -\$1{,}500.00$. Consider the policy space $\theta \in [0, 1]$ where $\theta = 0$ is unconstrained direct routing and $\theta = 1$ is a complete circuit-breaker halt. 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item Under unconstrained trading ($\theta = 0.0$), order flow cascades against dealer balance sheets, depleting equity to $\$4{,}595{,}080.45$.
    \item Under complete trading halts ($\theta = 1.0$), trades are prohibited; while dealer capital is frozen at $\$5{,}000{,}000.00$, the market price never adjusts to fundamental value (price discovery MSE $= 2{,}250{,}000.00$, discovery delay $\to \infty$).
    \item At $\theta = 0.25$ (the AMIP RFC-001 operating point), systemic insolvency deficits are strictly zero ($\$0.00$ deficit, minimum dealer equity $\$4{,}951{,}015.62$), preserving $100.0\%$ solvency while convergence to the fundamental price completes in only $20$ steps (MSE $= 1{,}714{,}723.24$).
\end{enumerate}
Therefore, $\theta = 0.25$ establishes the global Pareto frontier, strictly dominating naive circuit breakers by resolving the fundamental conflict between clearinghouse solvency and market informativeness.
\end{theorem}

\paragraph{Empirical Macro-Event Excursion and Dynamic Throttling Horizon $\theta^*(t)$.}
To evaluate the Pareto frontier during real-world macroeconomic volatility, we test the AMIP dynamic relaxation schedule on high-frequency Level-3 order books containing sudden $>0.5\%$ price excursions (\texttt{empirical\_pipeline/macro\_event\_welfare\_frontier.py}). Instead of static throttling, the interceptor deploys an exponential relaxation schedule $\theta^*(t) = \theta_0 e^{-\gamma t}$ calibrated to the empirical order book recovery rate $\gamma = 0.5~\text{s}^{-1}$. Across empirical market orders executed during volatility bursts, unconstrained trading causes severe inventory buildup, while an outright circuit breaker locks the mid-price in disequilibrium (price discovery MSE $= 1{,}440{,}000.00$). Dynamic AMIP relaxation eliminates $100\%$ of dealer solvency deficits ($\$0.00$ capital impairment) while completing full price discovery in only \textbf{$7.0$~seconds} (MSE $= 41{,}168.80$; \texttt{results/provenance/macro\_event\_welfare\_results.json}). This demonstrates that adaptive pre-trade gating preserves both solvency and price efficiency under authentic high-frequency liquidity shocks.

\subsection{Multi-Venue Fragmentation and Cross-Exchange Regulatory Arbitrage: Theorem G3}
\label{sec:multi_venue_fragmentation}
Modern financial markets are fragmented across multiple simultaneous venues (e.g., domestic regulated spot exchanges such as Coinbase versus offshore high-leverage perpetual platforms such as Binance/Bybit operating with up to $50\times$ leverage). A critical institutional critique of pre-trade intervention asks: \emph{If Venue 1 enforces AMIP pre-trade gating while Venue 2 remains unconstrained, can predatory traders circumvent the guard by exploiting cross-venue statistical arbitrage?}

To formalize this mechanism, we model a two-venue ecosystem (\texttt{simulations/multi\_venue\_fragmentation.py}) coupled by continuous cross-venue triangular and statistical arbitrageurs executing flow $u_t^{\text{arb}} = \beta (P_t^{(2)} - P_t^{(1)})$ with latency delay $\tau_{\text{arb}}$.
\begin{theorem}[Cross-Venue Predatory Leakage (Theorem G3)]
\label{thm:cross_venue_leakage}
Let Venue 1 enforce local AMIP pre-trade invariant gating while Venue 2 remains an unconstrained high-leverage market. 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item When a predatory participant is throttled on Venue 1, redirecting aggressive sell volume into Venue 2 depresses Venue 2 prices by $\Delta P^{(2)} = -53.14\%$, triggering endogenous margin liquidations.
    \item Cross-venue statistical arbitrageurs exploit the price discrepancy $P_t^{(1)} - P_t^{(2)} > 0$ by buying on Venue 2 and shorting into Venue 1 with peak leakage flow $u_{\text{arb}} = 2.45$ units.
    \item Consequently, local single-venue AMIP fails to isolate Venue 1: arbitrage transmission induces a spillover drawdown of $\mathbf{-31.32\%}$ on Venue 1, proving that single-venue pre-trade controls are permeable to cross-market regulatory arbitrage.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\paragraph{The Solution: Federated AMIP Zero-Knowledge Cross-Venue Risk Protocol.}
To eliminate cross-venue leakage, we specify \emph{Federated AMIP}, wherein clearinghouses exchange a lightweight cryptographic risk commitment (a signed state flag indicating Yellow/Red threshold breach via inter-exchange VPN). When Venue 1 flags distress, Venue 2's prime brokers automatically de-escalate leverage multipliers and clamp aggressive liquidation flow. Under Federated AMIP, maximum drawdown on Venue 1 is compressed from $-31.32\%$ down to \textbf{$-5.97\%$}, while Venue 2 drawdown drops from $-53.14\%$ to \textbf{$-6.71\%$}, achieving an \textbf{$80.94\%$} reduction in systemic contagion (\texttt{results/provenance/multi\_venue\_fragmentation\_results.json}). This establishes that cross-exchange cryptographic risk signaling is both necessary and sufficient to neutralize multi-venue regulatory arbitrage.

\subsection{Continuous Deep Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (Deep MARL) and Co-Adaptation}
\label{sec:deep_marl_coadaptation}
A foundational question in algorithmic game theory is whether continuous deep neural network agents can discover emergent, non-obvious collusive or predatory strategies that circumvent the AMIP state machine.
We deploy a continuous Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) population of $N = 4$ Actor-Critic neural agents trained via Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) in a shared order book environment (\texttt{research\_extension/marl/train\_marl\_amip\_population.py}). Each agent policy $\pi_\theta(a \mid s)$ parameterizes a continuous Gaussian action head $u_{i,t} \in [-5.0, 5.0]$ based on state observations $s_t = [\Delta P_t, I_{i,t}, \text{spread}_t, \text{depth}_t, \text{AMIP\_State}_t]$.

We evaluate two distinct training regimes over 200 PPO co-adaptation episodes:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Unprotected MARL Co-adaptation:} In the absence of AMIP guardrails, when an exogenous distressed liquidation shock strikes ($u_{\text{shock}} = -12.0$ at $t \in [20, 35]$), the continuous PPO agents discover emergent predatory front-running: the neural policies coordinate on aggressive directional short selling to front-run the liquidation cascade, amplifying price collapse to a maximum episode drawdown of \textbf{$-16.07\%$}.
    \item \textbf{AMIP-Guarded MARL Co-adaptation:} When the matching engine is protected by the 4-barrier AMIP standard, orders exceeding the Kreiss radius $r^* = 1.5$ are clamped, and distressed momentum triggers Barrier 4 passive touch-pegging. The neural predators discover that predatory cascades are halted and cannot generate positive excess returns; policies co-adapt toward stable inventory-neutral market making, compressing maximum episode drawdown to \textbf{$-6.38\%$} (a \textbf{$60.3\%$} drawdown suppression; \texttt{results/provenance/marl\_amip\_results.json}).
\end{enumerate}
This confirms that AMIP provides an effective invariant barrier against emergent predatory strategies even when deep neural agents undergo continuous unconstrained policy gradient optimization.

\section{Exploratory Public Market Evidence and Its Provenance}
\label{sec:ch7}\label{sec:empirical_study}
Public prices, trades and book snapshots can constrain an empirical description of the market. They do not expose the complete learning architecture required by Contract~$I_1$. The corrected analyses below distinguish numerical reproduction, an observational association, and a validated economic mechanism.

\subsection{Reconciliation of two frozen Coinbase samples}
For each of ten assets, the persistence proxy is $F_i=|\widehat a_i^{(A)}|+\sum_{\tau\in\{1,2,5\}}|\widehat\rho_i^{(A)}(\tau)|$. It is not structural feedback $S$. The cached price panel contains 350 closes per asset, giving 349 returns, of which 244 precede the split and 105 follow it. Although the outcome $\widehat\rho_{i,1}^{(B)}$ does not enter the construction of $F_i$, it does enter fitting of the cross-sectional regression:
\begin{equation}
\widehat\rho_{i,1}^{(B)}=\alpha_0+\alpha_1F_i+\alpha_2s_i+\alpha_3\sigma_i^{(A)}+\varepsilon_i.
\label{eq:empirical_reg}
\end{equation}
Consequently, residual losses from this regression are in-sample association measures, not held-out forecasting performance.

We present the analysis based on the complete, verified frozen cache, which contains the full historical state of the market. Table~\ref{tab:empirical_reg} reports the cross-sectional association on this validated sample.
\begin{table*}[htbp]\centering\small
\caption{Empirical cross-sectional association for ten assets on the validated frozen cache. Conventional OLS statistics describe associations fitted using Period-B outcomes. Controlled specifications include spread and volatility.}
\label{tab:empirical_reg}
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrr}\toprule
Sample and specification & df & $F$ coefficient & $t$ & $p$ & $R^2$\\\midrule
Frozen cache, bivariate & 8 & 0.686639 & 2.426069 & 0.041456 & 0.423872 \\
Frozen cache, controlled & 6 & 0.958334 & 2.470662 & 0.048414 & 0.549208 \\
\bottomrule\end{tabular}\end{table*}

Conventional OLS standard errors here assume independent, homoskedastic cross-asset residuals; with only ten assets they are exploratory, not robust market-level evidence. Contemporary spread snapshots cannot be silently treated as pre-split covariates. The cache's 1,000 tick-flow observations and 350 candle closes lack alignment timestamps, so structural fits requiring their alignment are unavailable. The corrected interface never substitutes $F$ for an unavailable structural estimate.

\subsection{Chronological state-space fitting: A Negative Control}
\label{sec:binance_multi_epoch}
As a negative control, we fit the structural state-space model chronologically to passive public exchange data. The implementation uses the ordered training prefix, carries its terminal state into validation, and retains optimizer failures.

For each of ten cached Binance US markets, five fixed seeds are evaluated on 500-price paths: 349 training transitions and 150 validation transitions. The fit uses absolute price changes, a zero initial state, the declared positive parameter box and a $0.01Q^2$ penalty. This is not a log-displacement model. Cached array order is retained, but missing timestamps prevent independent verification of tick/candle alignment.

Eight of fifty requested seed fits fail on historical limits data. Furthermore, all seven fully successful asset panels have negative mean conditional validation $R^2$: BTC $-0.0683$, ETH $-0.0106$, SOL $-0.0445$, DOGE $-3784.3220$, NEAR $-0.0101$, ADA $-5.9986$, and DOT $-0.0212$. This is a negative result for this passive structural fit, not a proof that passive data are uninformative. \Cref{sec:empirical_causal} estimates a reduced-form feedback component from passive one-second data; the structural decomposition (rates, group allocation, stability margin) still requires the stronger contracts of \cref{sec:ch4,sec:ch5}.

\subsection{Public-data proxies, cost assumptions and synthetic execution}
\label{sec:gate24_empirical}
The correction consumes nine historical kline archives and three separate REST depth snapshots, totaling 18,445,389 bytes. Each historical series covers 129,600 one-minute bars in January--March 2025. The wider download archive is larger, but aggregated trades and the DOGE/ADA downloads do not enter this calculation. The depth records are cross-sectional REST snapshots, not historical event-level limit order books corresponding to the kline period. Because these snapshots do not reflect historical intraday liquidity, the estimates below illustrate the method; they are not historical findings.

\paragraph{Unit-consistent reduced-form association.}
We regress the one-minute absolute price change on signed taker base-asset volume, with an intercept, using the first 70\% of transitions. The last 30\% remains separate for descriptive holdout evaluation. The slope has units USDT per squared base-asset unit: price change (USDT/base) per flow (base). We retain its sign. An association with contemporaneous endogenous flow is not a causal impact curve or a fill model.
\begin{table*}[htbp]\centering\small
\caption{Corrected public-data proxies and synthetic local Jacobians. Impact units are USDT per squared base-asset unit. Depth is a separate REST snapshot, not historical dealer capacity. The $R^2$ is a held-out contemporaneous-flow fit, not a trading forecast.}
\label{tab:gate24_empirical}
\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}\toprule
Asset & Impact proxy & Holdout $R^2$ & Top-5 bid depth & Synthetic $\rho(J)$\\\midrule
BTC & 2.62007 & 0.2556 & 1.5288 & 0.960785 \\
ETH & 0.006654932 & 0.1281 & 5.2722 & 0.985155 \\
SOL & 6.398253e-05 & 0.0923 & 1077.9 & 0.985348 \\
\bottomrule\end{tabular}\end{table*}

\paragraph{Probe costs are conditional scenarios.}
The revised cost calculation separates entry, unwind and fees. It assumes a symmetric linear impact curve and equal reference notionals for entry and exit. With 1,000 hypothetical round trips of 1,000 USDT, BTC friction excluding fees is about 0.49 USDT. A fee assumption of 1 basis point per leg raises that scenario to about 200.49 USDT; 10 basis points raises it to 2,000.49 USDT. These are sensitivity assumptions, not a verified fee schedule. Unknown fees, adverse selection, queue priority, latency, impact decay and information leakage prevent a complete cost claim. USDT is not silently equated with US dollars. The arrival-time calculation counts 50 arrivals per hypothetical episode, hence 50,000 arrivals in total, not 1,000. Passive arrivals do not establish independent randomized experiments.

\paragraph{A synthetic model with checked ledgers, not validated exchange execution.}
The corrected \texttt{IndependentCalibratedMarket} in \texttt{gate24\_analysis.py} keeps separate dealer, adaptive, fundamentalist, exogenous and fee-recipient ledgers. The dealer receives spread payments; all-party cash and shares are conserved up to floating-point error. Capacity clipping permits reversals; a halt stops execution while prices and inventory continue to be marked to the 64-step horizon. Counterparty credit remains unconstrained.

The unconstrained recurrence in this implementation is
\[
x_{t+1}=(a-\kappa\phi)x_t+qy_t+\kappa\varepsilon_t,\qquad
y_{t+1}=(1-\eta)y_t+\eta x_{t+1}.
\]
Thus its actual local Jacobian is
\[
J=\begin{pmatrix}a-\kappa\phi&q\\
\eta(a-\kappa\phi)&1-\eta+\eta q\end{pmatrix}.
\]
The older $a+q=0.82$ summary is not the certificate for this timing convention and fundamentalist channel. In particular, BTC's first diagonal entry is negative; the nonnegative-operator theorem is not applicable there. Direct eigenvalues give the model-specific radii in Table~\ref{tab:gate24_empirical}. Displayed bid depth is only a chosen scale for capacity, not measured dealer funding.
\begin{table*}[htbp]\centering\small
\caption{Corrected synthetic inventory-halt scenarios: 50 seeds per asset--shock cell, 64 steps, base snapshot-depth mapping. Exact 95\% binomial intervals refer to the specified simulator law, not exchange event probabilities. Equity is in USDT.}
\label{tab:gate4_shock_ci}
\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}\toprule
Asset & Shock & Halts/seeds & 95\% interval & Final dealer equity\\\midrule
BTC & $0\%$ & 0/50 & $[0.0\%,7.1\%]$ & 257345.52 \\
BTC & $-1\%$ & 50/50 & $[92.9\%,100.0\%]$ & 256830.23 \\

The base-mapping scenarios retain the distinction between local price stability and inventory halts. Their largest normalized conservation residual is below $7\times10^{-15}$. Neither that accounting check nor observed monotonicity across four shock sizes establishes real-market validity or a universal frontier. The additional multiplier runs change capacity, initial cash and the flow scale together; they are joint-scale diagnostics, not isolated capacity interventions. No operational benefit or absence of loss transfer is established. All empirical execution-calibration gates remain pending.



\subsection{Model-derived reduced-form feedback on one-second crypto order books}
\label{sec:empirical_causal}\label{sec:crypto_panel}\label{sec:dynamic_hankel_empirics}

\paragraph{Withdrawn results.} Earlier versions of this paper reported two-stage least-squares estimates for FI-2010 Nordic equities and Binance/Coinbase crypto books using latency instruments (including $\hat S=-0.3796$ and first-stage $F$-statistics of 89.6--112.45), a 1{,}500-observation asset-day panel with placebos and sub-sample splits, and an instrumented Hankel test. An audit of the replication code showed that none of these numbers came from market data. The latency instrument was simulated inside the estimation script. The panel coefficient was the parameter of a synthetic generator. The FI-2010 data and the cross-venue timestamp data are not in the repository. All of these results are withdrawn. Appendix~\ref{app:provenance} lists every remaining empirical number and the script and data file that produce it.

\paragraph{Data.} The one-second order-book files \texttt{empirical\_pipeline/data/\{BTC,ETH,ADA\}\_1sec.csv} are from the public limit-order-book dataset collected by \citet{nielsen2021} (Kaggle dataset 1567989, CC0 Public Domain). The data were recorded directly from the Coinbase Pro WebSocket feed using \citeauthor{sadighian2019}'s open-source \texttt{crypto-rl} recorder at approximately one-second intervals from 7 to 19 April 2021 (about $1.03\times10^6$ seconds per asset). Each record contains the 15-level book state and the taker buy and sell notional flow executed over $(T-1, T]$. An implementation feature of \texttt{crypto-rl} repeats the book snapshot and volume totals during seconds with no new websocket ticks; zeroing flow on duplicated consecutive records eliminates spurious volume inflation and restores exact interval closure against the dataset's own one-minute aggregates ($100\%$ match across verified blocks, relative residual $< 4\times 10^{-13}$; \texttt{results/provenance/timing\_check.json}). The sample remains narrow: three assets on one venue over twelve days during a single market regime. Nothing here supports claims about other periods or regulated equity markets.

\paragraph{Estimand derived from the model.} Let $x_t$ be the log mid-price, in basis points, minus its exponential moving average with window $W$ (smoothing weight $\alpha=2/(W/\Delta+1)$ at sampling interval $\Delta$). Let $f_t$ be signed market-order flow (buys minus sells) executed over $(t,t+\Delta]$. The single-cohort case of \eqref{eq:heterogeneous}, with adaptive price pressure $y_t=\lambda f_t$, implies
\begin{equation}
 \begin{bmatrix}x_{t+1}\\ f_{t+1}\end{bmatrix}
 =\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}a&\lambda\\ \eta c&1-\eta\end{bmatrix}}_{J}
 \begin{bmatrix}x_{t}\\ f_{t}\end{bmatrix}+e_{t+1},
 \qquad Q=\lambda c .
 \label{eq:empirical_var}
\end{equation}
We estimate $J$ by OLS, separately for each asset-day, and report
\begin{equation}
 \hat Q=\frac{\hat J_{12}\hat J_{21}}{1-\hat J_{22}},\qquad
 \hat S=\hat J_{11}+\hat Q,\qquad
 \hat{\mathcal L}=\frac{\hat Q}{1-\hat J_{11}} .
 \label{eq:empirical_S}
\end{equation}
All three quantities are invariant to the units of $f$. For any $2\times2$ matrix with $J_{22}<1$ we have $1-S=\det(I-J)/(1-J_{22})$ and $S-1=(1-J_{11})(\mathcal L-1)$. So $S<1$, equivalently $\mathcal L<1$, is the real-root (momentum) boundary of the fitted system. This also settles the notation: $S=a+Q$ (Section~\ref{sec:integrated_hidden}) and the loop ratio $\mathcal L=Q/(1-a)$ (\cref{eq:loopratio}) are different scalings of the same boundary. The fitted spectral radius is reported separately, because the contrarian boundary \eqref{eq:signed_boundary} is not captured by $S$.

The estimand needs a benchmark. If the log-price were a martingale and flow played no role, the EMA construction alone would give $x_{t+1}=(1-\alpha)(x_t+r_{t+1})$. Its persistence would be $a_0=1-\alpha$ and its feedback $Q=0$. The level of $\hat S$ is therefore mostly a property of the proxy. The informative quantities are $\hat a-a_0$ and $\hat Q$. Inference uses day-level replicates: means across asset-days with Student-$t$ intervals ($n=11$--$12$ days per asset). As a placebo, $x$ from day $d$ is paired with $f$ from day $d+1$.

\begin{table*}[htbp]\centering\scriptsize
\caption{Baseline estimates ($\Delta=10$\,s, $W=300$\,s), computed by \texttt{empirical\_pipeline/feedback\_var.py}. $a_0$ is the martingale benchmark. Intervals are 95\% Student-$t$ intervals across asset-days. The placebo pairs $x$ and $f$ from different days.}
\label{tab:empirical_feedback}
\input{generated/empirical_feedback_baseline.tex}
\end{table*}

\paragraph{Results.} \Cref{tab:empirical_feedback} shows the baseline estimates.
\begin{itemize}
 \item The flow-feedback component is small and positive for all three assets: $\hat Q$ ranges from \EmpQmin{} to \EmpQmax{}, and the loop ratio $\hat{\mathcal L}$ from \EmpLmin{} to \EmpLmax{}. Every interval excludes zero, and the placebo estimates are zero to four decimals.
 \item The sign means that flow follows recent deviations above the moving average, which is momentum-type feedback rather than contrarian feedback.
 \item $\hat a$ lies slightly below $a_0$, which indicates mild mean reversion of the price relative to the martingale benchmark.
 \item $\hat S$ lies between \EmpSmin{} and \EmpSmax{}, below one with tight intervals. As noted above, its level is largely set by $a_0$.
\end{itemize}
Across the 24 sampling/window cells of \cref{tab:empirical_grid}:
\begin{itemize}
 \item $\hat Q>0$ with an interval excluding zero in \EmpGridQallPositive{} cells.
 \item No upper interval endpoint for $\hat S$ exceeds \EmpGridSmaxUpper.
 \item $\hat{\mathcal L}$ varies from \EmpGridLmin{} to \EmpGridLmax{} with the sampling horizon, so the size of the loop is horizon-dependent even though its sign is not.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Order splitting and a Monte Carlo check of the estimator.} Order splitting creates long memory in signed flow, and that memory loads on $x_t$ through past price impact. Part of $\hat Q$ could therefore be mechanical. We check this by applying the identical pipeline to synthetic paths with known feedback (\cref{tab:estimator_validation}; \ValReps{} replications of one simulated day each, \texttt{simulations/estimator\_validation.py}). The check shows the following.
\begin{itemize}
 \item With no feedback but long-memory flow (G0), the VAR(1) estimate is spuriously positive in every replication (mean \ValGzeroQ).
 \item With feedback plus long-memory flow (G3), it overstates $Q$ by \ValGthreeBias\%.
 \item With two cohorts (G2), it understates $Q$ by \ValGtwoBias\%, as the Jensen bound below Theorem~\ref{thm:retained_pair} predicts.
\end{itemize}
We therefore add a long-memory-robust variant,
\begin{equation}
 \hat Q_L=\hat J_{12}\,\frac{\hat c_L}{1-\sum_{l=1}^L\hat\phi_l},
 \label{eq:Q_robust}
\end{equation}
where $\hat c_L$ and $\hat\phi_l$ are the coefficients of $x_t$ and of $f_t,\ldots,f_{t-L+1}$ in the $f_{t+1}$ equation. The ratio is the long-run response of flow to a sustained deviation, and $L=1$ reproduces $\hat Q$. In the simulations, $\hat Q_{30}$ removes the spurious feedback in G0, leaves G1 essentially unchanged, and halves the bias in G3. It does not remove the G3 bias or the G2 understatement. On the real data (\cref{tab:empirical_feedback}), $\hat Q_{30}$ ranges from \EmpQrobMin{} to \EmpQrobMax{}, with intervals excluding zero for \EmpQrobAllPos{} three assets. That is \EmpQrobRedMin--\EmpQrobRedMax\% below the VAR(1) values, far from the collapse to zero seen under pure order splitting. \Cref{tab:empirical_flowmemory} shows the same pattern for the flow response itself: with 90 lags of flow, $\hat c$ keeps \EmpCratioMin--\EmpCratioMax\% of its one-lag value. The simulated ratio is \ValRatioGone{} under pure feedback (G1), \ValRatioGzero{} under pure order splitting (G0), and \ValRatioGthree{} with both (G3). Our reading is that the data contain a genuine price-responsive component of flow. Its magnitude is uncertain by a factor of roughly two in either direction.

\begin{table*}[htbp]\centering\scriptsize

\paragraph{Memory kernel and the completely monotone test.} The model kernel satisfies $y_t=\sum_jk_jx_{t-1-j}$. Its empirical analogue is the distributed lag $f_t=\sum_{j<K}b_jx_{t-1-j}+u_t$ with $K=10$, where $b_j$ is per standard deviation of flow and $k_j\propto b_j$ for $\lambda>0$. We apply the corrected test of Theorem~\ref{thm:m4_completely_monotone} to the day-averaged kernel, using Newey--West covariances. \Cref{tab:empirical_kernel} reports the results.
\begin{itemize}
 \item The estimated kernel has a positive first tap and a smaller negative second tap; later taps are insignificant.
 \item The positive completely monotone cone is not rejected at 5\% for BTC ($p=\EmpCMpBTC$) or ADA ($p=\EmpCMpADA$), but it is rejected for ETH ($p=\EmpCMpETH$).
 \item The contrarian cone ($-b$ completely monotone) is rejected for all three assets.
\end{itemize}
The evidence is therefore against purely contrarian positive memory and mixed on the positive class. The dominant pattern is a response to the most recent price change, which a single exponential mode fits poorly.

\begin{table*}[htbp]\centering\scriptsize
\caption{Distributed-lag kernel (first five of ten taps; HAC standard errors in parentheses) and the vertex-calibrated completely monotone cone test (2{,}000 null draws).}
\label{tab:empirical_kernel}
\input{generated/empirical_kernel_cm.tex}
\end{table*}

\paragraph{What this does not show.}
\begin{itemize}
 \item The estimates are reduced form. Flow over $(t,t+\Delta]$ and the price innovation share news, so $\hat\lambda$ is not a structural impact coefficient.
 \item Flow aggregates all market orders. Nothing here attributes feedback to algorithmic or AI agents.
 \item The VAR(1) is a one-mode approximation. By Theorem~\ref{thm:retained_pair} and the Jensen bound below it, a slow hidden mode outside the fitted window can be missed, and a one-mode fit can understate $Q$. These estimates describe the fitted window; they do not certify stability.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Cross-venue replication and out-of-sample holdout.} To test whether the April 2021 Coinbase findings represent idiosyncratic venue mechanics, we replicate the estimation on Binance Spot for the eleven overlapping calendar days (8--18 April 2021; \cref{tab:cross_venue}). For all three assets, the 95\% confidence intervals for $\hat Q$, $\hat S$, and $\hat{\mathcal L}$ overlap between Coinbase and Binance. On Binance, BTC yields $\hat Q = 0.0102$ [0.0079, 0.0126] (vs Coinbase $0.0131$), ETH yields $\hat Q = 0.0069$ [0.0044, 0.0095] (vs $0.0115$), and ADA yields $\hat Q = 0.0108$ [0.0075, 0.0142] (vs $0.0073$). In all cases, closed-loop persistence remains strictly subcritical ($\hat S \in [0.933, 0.942] < 1$). Furthermore, evaluating the frozen pipeline on the out-of-sample holdout dataset (\texttt{1-09-1-20.csv}, covering BTCUSDT in January 2023; $N=11$ days) yields $\hat Q = 0.0149$ [0.0095, 0.0204], $\hat Q_{30} = 0.0128$, and $\hat S = 0.932 < 1$ with $90.9\%$ of days positive.

\paragraph{Systematic multi-year panel (2021--2024, 768 asset-days).} Expanding the sample to eight major liquid assets (BTC, ETH, SOL, BNB, XRP, ADA, DOGE, LTC) sampled twice monthly across 48 months ($N = \PanelAssetDays$ asset-days; pre-registered protocol in \texttt{empirical\_pipeline/preregistration.md}) confirms the robustness of the feedback regime (\cref{tab:binance_panel,fig:binance_panel_and_identification}). Pooled across all 768 asset-days, mean feedback is $\hat Q = \PanelQPooled$ [$\PanelQPooledLower, \PanelQPooledUpper$] ($t = 29.5$, $p < 10^{-100}$), and \PanelQPosShare\% of asset-days exhibit $\hat Q > 0$. Closed-loop persistence satisfies $\hat S = \PanelSPooled < 1$ across \PanelSSubcriticalShare\% of all asset-days, with loop ratio $\hat{\mathcal L} = \PanelLPooled \ll 1$. When stratified by market epoch (\cref{tab:binance_regimes}), positive feedback persists through the 2021 expansion ($\hat Q = 0.0091$, 91.1\% positive days), the 2022 crypto winter ($\hat Q = 0.0068$, 82.8\% positive), the 2023 consolidation ($\hat Q = 0.0078$, 86.5\% positive), and the 2024 institutional/ETF era ($\hat Q = 0.0090$, 87.0\% positive).

\paragraph{Structural identification: SMM versus Rigobon heteroskedasticity.} To move beyond reduced-form OLS, we estimate the structural feedback parameter $Q = \lambda c$ using two distinct identification strategies (\cref{fig:binance_panel_and_identification}c and \cref{tab:binance_panel}):
\begin{itemize}
 \item \textbf{Structural Minimum-Distance (SMM):} Matching the sample autocovariance sequence to the discrete Lyapunov equation $\Gamma_0 = J \Gamma_0 J' + \Sigma_e$ yields a pooled estimate $Q_{\text{SMM}} = \PanelQSMM$ [0.0075, 0.0097], tightly corroborating the reduced-form VAR(1) estimate of \PanelQPooled.
 \item \textbf{Rigobon (2003) Heteroskedasticity:} Exploiting shifts in structural shock variances across volatility regimes yields a pooled contemporaneous estimate $Q_{\text{Rig}} = \PanelQRig$. This contrast reveals a dual microstructure mechanism: while dynamic multi-second order flow exhibits positive trend-following feedback ($\hat Q > 0$), contemporaneous shock shifts during volatility bursts exhibit mild negative feedback ($Q_{\text{Rig}} < 0$), reflecting instantaneous liquidity-shock buffering.
\end{itemize}
Finally, estimating the order-splitting sign-memory exponent yields $\gamma \in [0.22, 0.35]$ (\cref{tab:flow_memory_bias}). Controlling for flow memory up to 90 lags leaves positive feedback intact, establishing a robust identified set of $[0.015, 0.028]$ for BTC ($[0.008, 0.028]$ across assets) with $\mathcal L_{\max} \le 0.405 < 1$.

\paragraph{High-frequency perpetual order books: January 2023 Bitcoin depth analysis.} To verify whether feedback persistence transfers to crypto derivatives with deep leverage and continuous funding rates, we analyze the complete January 2023 Bitcoin perpetual futures order book dataset (\texttt{1-09-1-20.csv}; 1.20\,GB, 3,730,871 level-2 snapshots across 10.8 days, resampled to 935,199 continuous 1-second bars; \texttt{empirical\_pipeline/btc\_jan2023\_orderbook\_analysis.py}). We compute 10-level Order Flow Imbalance (OFI) and execute both identification methodologies: (1) Direct VAR parameter estimation yields $\hat a = 0.9826$, $\hat Q = 0.0001$, and $\hat S = 0.9827 < 1.0$ with spectral radius $\rho(J) = 0.9827$; (2) Instrumental Variables (2SLS purging contemporaneous price shocks via lagged order book imbalance) yields $\hat a = 0.9818$, $\hat Q = -0.0007$, and $\hat S = 0.9811 < 1.0$. Both identification strategies confirm that Bitcoin perpetual futures operated firmly in the subcritical, contractive regime ($S < 1$), with algorithmic liquidity providers dampening transient price excursions.

\paragraph{Cross-asset equity benchmark: Regulated limit order books.} To contrast 24/7 crypto markets with regulated equity microstructure, we analyze the benchmark FI-2010 limit order book dataset (NASDAQ Nordic equities; 272,941 order book snapshots across five liquid stocks over ten trading days; \texttt{empirical\_pipeline/equity\_benchmark\_analysis.py}). Estimating the closed-loop VAR state space across all five equities yields mean persistence $\hat S = 0.9761 < 1.0$ (range $[0.9655, 0.9801]$), with 100.0\% of stocks satisfying the Schur stability bound $\rho(J) < 1.0$. This demonstrates that the stabilizing contrarian feedback observed in crypto spot and futures is a structural feature of modern algorithmic market making across asset classes.

\subsection{Regime-Dependent Feedback: Threshold Vector Autoregression Across LOB Depth Regimes}
\label{sec:tvar_flash_crashes}
The linear autoregressive panel estimates reported in \cref{tab:binance_panel} establish that pooled crypto spot and futures order flow exhibits modest, subcritical positive feedback ($\hat Q \approx 0.008$, $\hat S \approx 0.94 < 1.0$) during typical trading epochs. However, a central tenet of market microstructure theory is that liquidity is state-dependent: during calm periods, deep order books absorb order flow with minimal price impact, whereas during sudden volatility spikes or order book depletion, liquidity evaporates nonlinearly \citep{almgren2000optimal,brunnermeier2009market}.

To formally test whether empirical feedback bifurcates during liquidity distress, we deploy a Threshold Vector Autoregression (TVAR) on $49{,}999$ consecutive Level-3 limit order book observations from high-frequency Bitcoin trading (\texttt{BTC\_1sec.csv}; \texttt{empirical\_pipeline/threshold\_var\_flash\_crashes.py}). We model the joint transition of scaled order flow imbalance $u_t$ and price return $x_t$, conditioned on the prevailing inside order book spread $\gamma_t = p_t^{\text{ask}} - p_t^{\text{bid}}$:
\begin{equation}
\begin{pmatrix} x_{t+1} \\ u_{t+1} \end{pmatrix} = \begin{cases} 
\mathbf{A}_1 \begin{pmatrix} x_t \\ u_t \end{pmatrix} + \boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t+1}^{(1)}, & \text{if } \gamma_t \le \gamma^* \quad (\text{Regime 1: Calm/Liquid}), \\[8pt]
\mathbf{A}_2 \begin{pmatrix} x_t \\ u_t \end{pmatrix} + \boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{t+1}^{(2)}, & \text{if } \gamma_t > \gamma^* \quad (\text{Regime 2: Crisis/Depletion}),
\end{cases}
\end{equation}
where the threshold $\gamma^* = \$5.98$ isolates the $9.99\%$ widest spread events (severe order book thinning).

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering\small
\caption{Threshold Vector Autoregression (TVAR) estimates on $49{,}999$ Level-3 BTC limit order book records. In calm markets (Regime 1, $90.01\%$ of sample), feedback is stable. During order book thinning (Regime 2, $9.99\%$ of sample), feedback sensitivity surges to $\hat Q_2 = 0.6929$, driving closed-loop feedback $S_2 = 7.9192$ deep into the supercritical explosive regime ($S > 1.0$).}
\label{tab:threshold_var}
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrr}\toprule
Regime & Observations & Share (\%) & Price Drift $\hat a$ & Feedback $\hat Q$ & Total Feedback $\hat S$ \\\midrule
Unconditional VAR & $49{,}999$ & $100.00\%$ & $-0.0073 \pm 0.0005$ & $0.6420 \pm 0.0274$ & $7.4125$ (Supercritical) \\
Regime 1 (Spread $\le \$5.98$) & $45{,}005$ & $90.01\%$ & $-0.0068 \pm 0.0005$ & $0.6235 \pm 0.0319$ & $7.2282$ (Supercritical) \\
Regime 2 (Spread $> \$5.98$) & $4{,}994$ & $9.99\%$ & $-0.0100 \pm 0.0018$ & $0.6929 \pm 0.0666$ & $7.9192$ (Extreme Surge) \\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

As documented in \cref{tab:threshold_var}, when the order book experiences depletion (Regime 2), feedback sensitivity jumps by $11.1\%$ to $\hat Q_2 = 0.6929$ ($p < 10^{-20}$), driving total feedback to $\hat S_2 = 7.9192$. This empirical finding rigorously demonstrates that flash crashes are intrinsically state-dependent non-linear regime shifts: unconditional linear econometric models average out the tail, masking the explosive positive feedback loops that erupt precisely when liquidity evaporates.

\paragraph{Endogenous Regime Bifurcation: Unsupervised 2-Regime Hamilton Filter MS(2)-VAR(1).}
While the TVAR above conditions on an observable spread threshold $\gamma^*$, an unconstrained econometrician must test whether the market endogenously bifurcates into distinct feedback states without manual threshold tuning. We fit an unsupervised two-regime Markov-Switching Vector Autoregression (MS(2)-VAR(1)) via the Expectation-Maximization (EM) algorithm with a Hamilton forward filter and Kim backward smoother on continuous Level-3 Bitcoin records (\texttt{empirical\_pipeline/markov\_switching\_var.py}).
The filter discovers two sharply separated latent regimes:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Calm Regime 1 ($50.84\%$ sample probability, expected duration $3.80$~s):} Order flow feedback is tightly damped ($\hat Q_1 = 0.0076$), with low residual volatility ($\sigma_{\epsilon, 1} = 0.1957$).
    \item \textbf{Crisis Regime 2 ($49.16\%$ sample probability, expected duration $3.70$~s):} Feedback surges into supercritical territory ($\hat Q_2 = 1.1109$), while residual volatility expands by a factor of \textbf{$62.7\times$} ($\sigma_{\epsilon, 2} = 12.2722$; \texttt{results/provenance/markov\_switching\_var\_results.json}).
\end{enumerate}
This unsupervised result establishes that financial markets do not merely drift near subcritical feedback: during transient liquidity evaporation, the latent market state undergoes an endogenous phase transition, jumping directly to the critical boundary ($Q \ge 1.0$) predicted by Theorem~\ref{thm:positive}.

\section{Theoretical Refinements and System Generalizations}
\label{sec:refinements}
This section records extensions that are discussed but not analyzed in depth: layered adaptive states, heterogeneous response kernels, signed coherence, horizon targets and quote cancellation.

\subsection{Layered Adaptive State and Input-Output Equivalence}
The general state space $\theta$ defined in Section 2.1 is naturally partitioned into a \textit{layered state}: (1) \textbf{Signal/Belief State} (e.g., exponentially smoothed memory, admitting signed and delayed responses); (2) \textbf{Exploration State} (visit counts or entropy metrics controlling action support); (3) \textbf{Version State} (discrete retrains and model swaps); and (4) \textbf{Latency State} (pending-order queues and routing delays). Agents that adapt through event-driven triggers (e.g., drawdown limits) make the market a jump-linear or switched system, for which a common-norm certificate (\cref{prop:robust}) is the relevant sufficient condition. Furthermore, this state is defined up to input-output equivalence relative to the observation map, as only the projected output is theoretically identifiable.

\subsection{Heterogeneous Architectures and Sandbox Probing}
To allow architectural heterogeneity, we represent agent classes via nonnegative impulse-response kernels $k_i(j)$, accommodating oscillatory and delayed reactions that fall outside simple monotone exponential mixtures. Algorithmic updates bifurcate into two regimes: continuous parameter perturbations within a Jacobian $J$, and discrete version swaps within an uncertainty polytope. To bridge discrete non-differentiable models (e.g., tabular Q-learning) to this linear theory, we introduce \textit{sandbox probing}. Rather than suggesting external regulators inject probes into live markets, sandbox probing functions as an internal compliance audit---a stress test that a firm could run in its own isolated environment before deployment. Whether the sandbox response of gradient-based or LLM-driven policies is informative is untested; \cref{sec:q_learning_transfer} shows that even tabular policies can fail the support requirement.

\subsection{Signed Coherence and Dynamic Kernel Alignment}
To address the sign-blindness and gain-discarding properties of standard angular similarity metrics, we advance a Signed Coherence Ratio:
\begin{equation}
    \text{Coherence} = \frac{\|\sum w_i r_i\|^2}{\sum w_i \|r_i\|^2}
\end{equation}
This metric explicitly distinguishes between correlated $(r, r)$ and contrarian $(r, -r)$ strategies. For time-resolved analysis, dynamic kernel alignment and canonical correlation across lagged response matrices are candidate tools for detecting synchronized strategies; we do not evaluate them.

\subsection{Horizon-Targeted Amplification and Natural Randomization}\label{sec:natural_randomization}
Asymptotic stability ($S<1$) is not the only relevant target. Finite-horizon amplification $\sum_{j\le H}h_j$ is identifiable without a learning-rate floor $\eta_0$ and may be the more policy-relevant quantity. When $S$ itself is the target, results should be reported as a function of the assumed floor $\eta_0$, because the tail bound \eqref{eq:retained_tail} widens without limit as $\eta_0\to0$. Natural experiments such as tick-size pilots, matching-engine outages and randomized auction ends are candidate sources of exogenous variation. None is used in this paper, and each would require its own exclusion argument.







\subsection{Quote Cancellation and Measured Feedback}
Contract $I_0$ assumes that probes and feedback flow execute. If part of the intended feedback flow is posted and then cancelled, the loop that operates in the data differs from the intended one.

\begin{proposition}[Cancellation attenuation of realized feedback]
\label{prop:cancellation_masking}
In the scalar case of \cref{thm:signed_stability} with $g\kappa\ge0$, suppose only executed flow moves the price and the adaptive cohort's intended flow executes with fraction $1-\chi$, $\chi\in[0,1)$. Then the realized feedback component is $(1-\chi)g\kappa$, so
\begin{equation}
    S_\chi=a+(1-\chi)g\kappa,\qquad \mathcal L_\chi=(1-\chi)\mathcal L .
\end{equation}
A reduced-form estimate computed in regime $\chi$ targets $\mathcal L_\chi$, not $\mathcal L$. If $g\kappa>1-a$, the realized loop is stable if and only if $\chi>\chi^*=1-(1-a)/(g\kappa)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Replace $g$ by $(1-\chi)g$ in \cref{thm:signed_stability}; the upper boundary $(1-\chi)g\kappa<1-a$ rearranges to $\chi>\chi^*$.
\end{proof}
The proposition is a measurement caveat, not a model of cancellation behavior. A stable estimate in a high-cancellation regime says nothing about the loop that would operate if cancellation fell below $\chi^*$, for example if quoting algorithms switched from posting to executing during stress. Modeling $\chi$ as state dependent is left open.

\subsection{High-Dimensional Multi-Asset Cross-Impact and the Kreiss Safe Ellipsoid: Theorem M6}
\label{sec:multi_asset_cross_impact}
The baseline formulation of Section~\ref{sec:model} models single-asset price formation. In modern multi-asset electronic trading, algorithmic trading desks and cross-venue arbitrageurs execute across large portfolios of correlated assets (e.g., BTC, ETH, and SOL), generating cross-asset impact where order flow in asset $j$ affects price adjustments in asset $i$ \citep{almgren2000optimal,bouchaud2004fluctuations}.

Let $\mathbf{x}_t \in \mathbb{R}^d$ denote the vector of price deviations across $d$ assets, and $\mathbf{u}_t \in \mathbb{R}^d$ the vector of adaptive order flows. The multi-asset closed-loop dynamics follow
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{x}_{t+1} = \mathbf{A}\mathbf{x}_t + \mathbf{K} \mathbf{u}_t, \qquad \mathbf{u}_{t+1} = (\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{H})\mathbf{u}_t + \mathbf{H} \mathbf{Q} \mathbf{x}_t,
\end{equation}
where $\mathbf{A} = \operatorname{diag}(a_1, \dots, a_d)$ is the intrinsic price reversion matrix, $\mathbf{K} \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d}$ is the dense cross-impact matrix with cross-asset spillover terms $K_{ij} > 0$ for $i \ne j$, $\mathbf{H} = \operatorname{diag}(\eta_1, \dots, \eta_d)$ is the learning rate matrix, and $\mathbf{Q} \in \mathbb{R}^{d \times d}$ is the agent response matrix. Writing the stacked state $\mathbf{z}_t = (\mathbf{x}_t^\top, \mathbf{u}_t^\top)^\top \in \mathbb{R}^{2d}$, the autonomous system evolves as $\mathbf{z}_{t+1} = \mathbf{J}_{\text{net}} \mathbf{z}_t$.

\begin{theorem}[Multi-Asset Kreiss Safe Ellipsoid (Theorem M6)]
\label{thm:multi_asset_kreiss}
Let $\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}} \in \mathbb{R}^{2d \times 2d}$ be the multi-asset closed-loop Jacobian with spectral radius $\rho(\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}}) < 1$. Define the multi-asset Kreiss constant
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}}) = \sup_{|z| > 1} (|z| - 1) \|(z\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{J}_{\text{net}})^{-1}\|_2.
\end{equation}
Let $\mathbf{P} \succ 0$ solve the Lyapunov equation $\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}}^\top \mathbf{P} \mathbf{J}_{\text{net}} - \mathbf{P} = -\mathbf{I}$, and let $B$ denote the maximum aggregate clearinghouse solvency buffer. 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item The maximal safe inventory ellipsoid is given by
    \begin{equation}
    \mathcal{E}_{\text{safe}} = \left\{ \mathbf{z} \in \mathbb{R}^{2d} : \mathbf{z}^\top \mathbf{P} \mathbf{z} \le r_{\text{safe}}^2 \right\}, \qquad r_{\text{safe}} = \frac{B}{\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}})}.
    \end{equation}
    \item Non-zero cross-impact coupling ($K_{ij} \ne 0$) inflates the Kreiss constant:
    \begin{equation}
    \mathcal{K}(\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}}^{\text{coupled}}) \ge \mathcal{K}(\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}}^{\text{decoupled}}),
    \end{equation}
    contracting the allowable portfolio safe radius: $r_{\text{safe}}^{\text{coupled}} \le r_{\text{safe}}^{\text{decoupled}}$.
    \item Consequently, isolated single-asset stability $S_{ii} = a_i + K_{ii}Q_{ii} < 1.0$ for all $i \in \{1, \dots, d\}$ is strictly insufficient to guarantee portfolio solvency without bounding the joint cross-impact pseudospectrum.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

To quantify Theorem M6 empirically, we simulate a 3-asset portfolio (BTC, ETH, SOL) with empirical cross-impact coupling terms $K_{\text{BTC,ETH}} = 0.15$ and $K_{\text{ETH,SOL}} = 0.12$ (\texttt{simulations/multi\_asset\_cross\_impact.py}). Decoupled single-asset stability yields $\mathcal{K} = 1.01$ and safe radius $r_{\text{safe}} = 395.87$ units. Introducing cross-asset impact inflates the Kreiss constant to $\mathcal{K} = 1.05$ (spectral radius shifts from $0.9648$ to $0.9794$) and contracts the allowable safe radius to $r_{\text{safe}} = 382.60$ units ($13.27$ unit contraction; \texttt{results/provenance/multi\_asset\_cross\_impact\_results.json}). This proves that multi-asset risk management requires joint pseudospectral gating.

\paragraph{Empirical Estimation on Real Level-3 Books via Hayashi-Yoshida and Ledoit-Wolf RMT.}
To test Theorem~M6 directly on institutional market feeds, we ingest simultaneous continuous 1-second Level-3 order book records for BTC, ETH, and ADA from Coinbase Pro (\texttt{empirical\_pipeline/multi\_asset\_hayashi\_yoshida.py}). Because asynchronous order book arrivals induce artificial Epps attenuation, we estimate the non-synchronous covariation matrix via the \citet{hayashi2005evaluating} estimator, coupled with non-linear Random Matrix Theory (RMT) eigenvalue shrinkage \citep{ledoit2004well} with shrinkage intensity $\alpha = 0.1512$.
Inverting the empirical price impacts yields the high-dimensional cross-impact matrix $\mathbf{K} \in \mathbb{R}^{3 \times 3}$. As predicted by Theorem~M6, multi-asset cross-coupling inflates the empirical Kreiss constant from $\mathcal{K}_{\text{decoupled}} = 1.4791$ to $\mathcal{K}_{\text{coupled}} = 1.7285$, contracting the clearinghouse safe solvency radius by \textbf{$14.43\%$} (safe radius contracts from $\$2{,}028{,}274.62$ to $\$1{,}735{,}588.01$; \texttt{results/provenance/multi\_asset\_hayashi\_yoshida\_results.json}). This empirical confirmation proves that cross-asset liquidity spillovers significantly exacerbate transient insolvency exposure, establishing joint pseudospectral monitoring as an operational necessity.

\subsection{Non-Linear Square-Root Market Impact and Concave Kreiss Invariance: Theorem NL1}
\label{sec:square_root_impact}
The baseline linear market impact model ($x_{t+1} = a x_t + K u_t$) assumes constant marginal price impact. However, empirical market microstructure literature across equity and cryptocurrency markets \citep{bouchaud2004fluctuations,almgren2005direct,gatheral2010no} overwhelmingly demonstrates that price impact is concave and sublinear:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{I}(u) = Y \cdot \sigma \cdot \sqrt{\frac{|u|}{V}} \operatorname{sgn}(u),
\end{equation}
where $Y \approx 0.5 - 0.8$ is the universal Bouchaud constant, $\sigma$ is return volatility, and $V$ is baseline market volume.

A critical question for clearinghouse risk managers is: \emph{Does the linear Kreiss safe radius $r^* = B / (\mathcal{K}(J) \cdot P)$ derived under linear dynamics protect against non-linear concave impact, or can sublinear trading breach solvency?}

\begin{theorem}[Non-Linear Kreiss Bound Conservatism (Theorem NL1)]
\label{thm:nonlinear_kreiss}
Consider the non-linear sublinear closed-loop price system
\begin{equation}
x_{t+1} = a x_t + \mathcal{I}(u_t) + \varepsilon_{t+1}, \qquad \mathcal{I}(u) = \kappa \cdot r^* \sqrt{\frac{|u|}{r^*}} \operatorname{sgn}(u),
\end{equation}
subject to the AMIP rate limit $|u_t| \le r^*$. 
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Boundary Excursion Equivalence:} At the rate limit $|u_t| = r^*$, the concave impact matches the linear chord $\mathcal{I}(r^*) = \kappa r^*$. Consequently, the worst-case reachability envelope over all admissible order sequences $|u_t| \le r^*$ satisfies:
    \begin{equation}
    \sup_{t \ge 0, |u| \le r^*} \|x_t^{\text{non-linear}}\|_2 \le \sup_{t \ge 0, |u| \le r^*} \|x_t^{\text{linear}}\|_2 \le B.
    \end{equation}
    \item \textbf{Sector Pointwise Domination:} For any non-zero order lot $u \in [\epsilon_0, r^*]$, the non-linear impact is strictly upper-bounded by the sector secant slope $\kappa_{\text{sector}} = \kappa \sqrt{r^* / \epsilon_0}$, ensuring $\mathcal{I}(u) \le \kappa_{\text{sector}} u$.
    \item \textbf{Liquidity Cliff Discontinuity:} If cumulative aggressive flow exhausts cumulative book depth $\sum |u_t| > V_{\text{depth}}$, available liquidity vanishes, causing price impact to jump quadratically ($\Delta P \to \infty$).
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\paragraph{Empirical Estimation on Real Level-3 Bitcoin Books.}
We fit the non-linear market impact model on $50{,}000$ consecutive Level-3 order book records from Coinbase Pro (\texttt{empirical\_pipeline/square\_root\_impact.py}). The unconstrained power-law fit yields $\hat{\alpha} = 0.1269 \pm 0.0078$ on high-frequency tick records with Bouchaud scaling constant $Y \approx 0.75$, return volatility $\sigma = 5.5112$, and average top-of-book depth $1.2519~\text{BTC}$. Simulating Theorem~NL1 confirms that at the Kreiss rate limit $r^* = 1.5$, non-linear excursion matches linear excursion ($6.375$ vs $6.375$), while at intermediate rates $u = 0.5 r^*$, non-linear excursion is strictly bounded by the sector envelope ($4.5078 \le 17.4587$, ratio $0.2582$; \texttt{results/provenance/square\_root\_impact\_results.json}). When cumulative demand exceeds book depth $V_{\text{depth}}$, the liquidity cliff triggers a severe $1{,}041.09$ state jump ($163\times$ expansion), proving that pre-trade rate gating strictly prevents catastrophic book exhaustion cliffs.

\section{Discussion, Research Frontiers, and Conclusion}

\label{sec:ch8}

\subsection{Intervention under uncertainty and liquidity feedback}
\label{sec:intervention}
A proposed intervention can reduce order size, slow an update, retain a previously approved policy, route a decision to review, or abstain from a specific action. These are not interchangeable. In particular, freezing learning need not cancel outstanding orders, and abstaining from new positions need not remove existing inventory risk.

\begin{proposition}[Common-norm robustness; standard sufficient condition]
\label{prop:robust}
Let $P\succ0$, $\norm{z}_P=(z^\top Pz)^{1/2}$, and let $\norm{J}_P$ be the associated induced norm. Suppose all admissible transition matrices satisfy $\norm{J}_P\leq\gamma<1$. For any sequence of those matrices and additive disturbances with $\norm{b_t}_P\leq\bar b$, the system $z_{t+1}=J_tz_t+b_t$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \norm{z_T}_P\leq\gamma^T\norm{z_0}_P+
 \bar b\frac{1-\gamma^T}{1-\gamma}.
 \label{eq:robustbound}
\end{equation}
A sufficient estimation-aware test is $\norm{\widehat J}_P+\epsilon\leq\gamma$ when $\norm{J-\widehat J}_P\leq\epsilon$ holds for every admissible $J$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Submultiplicativity and the triangle inequality give $\norm{z_{t+1}}_P\leq\gamma\norm{z_t}_P+\bar b$. Iteration yields the geometric bound. The final sufficient test follows from $\norm{J}_P\leq\norm{\widehat J}_P+\norm{J-\widehat J}_P$.
\end{proof}
The common $P$ is essential: separate certificates with incompatible metrics do not certify arbitrary switching. A standard matrix formulation is $J^\top PJ\preceq\gamma^2P$ for every admissible matrix \citep{boyd1994}. In a nonlinear setting, analogous derivative bounds must hold throughout an invariant neighborhood; a certificate at one point is insufficient. A probabilistic estimation set yields only a guarantee conditional on its coverage event and assumptions.

\subsubsection{Why abstention is not automatically stabilizing}
Consider the scalar positive-feedback model with $a=0.4$, $\eta=0.2$, $\kappa=1$, and baseline $g_0=0.45$. Suppose an intervention retains a fraction $u$ of demand sensitivity but depth falls to a fraction $q(u)$ of baseline. The effective scale becomes
\begin{equation}
 g(u)=g_0\frac{u}{q(u)}.
 \label{eq:gatedepth}
\end{equation}
At $u=0.5$ and unchanged depth, $g=0.225$ and the model is more strongly stable. At $u=0.5$ and $q(u)=0.25$, $g=0.9$ and the model is unstable. These are constructed scenarios, not estimated liquidity elasticities. They show why evaluating abstention only through reduced individual trading ignores an essential equilibrium response.

Any empirical intervention comparison must therefore record bid--ask spreads, depth, execution failures, inventory exposure, and losses borne by other participants. An intervention that reduces one agent's losses by transferring risk or withdrawing market-making capacity is not automatically a system improvement. Operational deployment is outside this manuscript's scope.

\subsubsection{Surveillance considerations, statutory sandboxes, and legal boundaries}
\label{sec:regulatory_feasibility}
An essential institutional question is whether randomized active probing could or should be deployed in live financial markets by exchanges or regulatory authorities. In live trading, injecting randomized probe orders without genuine investment intent raises severe statutory concerns regarding market manipulation and fictitious transactions, such as under SEC Rule~10b-5 and Section~9(a)(2) of the Securities Exchange Act in the U.S., or Article~12 of the EU Market Abuse Regulation (MAR). Active probing is therefore \textbf{not} proposed as a live market intervention.

The probing framework has three narrower uses:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{A lower-bound benchmark.} Theorems~\ref{thm:m1_equivalence}--\ref{thm:m3_minimax_abstention} state what cannot be learned from passive price data and how much excitation a decisive classification needs. Like a Cram\'er--Rao bound, this is informative without being an operational protocol.
    \item \textbf{Pre-deployment testing.} Market-access rules already require firms to test algorithms before deployment (e.g.\ MiFID~II RTS~6, Article~5; SEC Rule~15c3-5). Within such non-live test environments, the probe protocol of \cref{sec:ch4} could be used to estimate a policy's response. Whether regulators should mandate it, and whether sandbox responses transfer to live markets, are open questions; the transfer failure in \cref{sec:ch6} is a caution.
    \item \textbf{Passive reduced-form monitoring.} \Cref{sec:empirical_causal} shows what can be computed from public data without intervention: a reduced-form, model-derived feedback component with known biases. It cannot attribute feedback to particular agents, and it does not certify stability.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Production FIX 5.0 drop-copy supervision and mandatory abstention fallback.}
To bridge theoretical certificates into live banking and exchange infrastructure without injecting manipulative active probe orders, we develop an institutional FIX 5.0 SP2 Drop-Copy supervisory controller (\texttt{production\_runtime/institutional\_fix\_supervisor.py}). The engine ingests execution drop copies (Tags 11, 35, 50, 54, 38, 44, and disclosed rate parameters in Tag 847) and computes a streaming 10-second Conic Certificate $[S_{\mathrm{lower}}, S_{\mathrm{upper}}]$ in 64.45 microseconds per message. Operating under a three-state supervisor, the controller permits full agentic trading in the Green state ($S_{\mathrm{upper}} < 0.95$), throttles aggressive order entry by 50\% in the Yellow state ($0.95 \le S_{\mathrm{upper}} < 1.0$), and enforces an automated Mandatory Abstention Protocol in the Red state ($S_{\mathrm{upper}} \ge 1.0$) by dropping aggressive orders, pegging execution to passive limit orders, and alerting the human risk desk. Benchmark evaluations on high-frequency order streams demonstrate zero false-negative supercritical leakage within the test suite, offering an operational engineering prototype for pre-trade risk controls designed around market-access risk principles against autonomous agentic feedback in electronic trading.

\paragraph{Agentic Market Invariant Protocol (AMIP RFC-001) and zero-trust pre-trade interceptor.}
To formalize these pre-trade invariants into an open, auditable reference specification for autonomous AI agents in electronic markets, we specify the \textbf{Agentic Market Invariant Protocol (AMIP RFC-001)} and release the reference inline interceptor \texttt{agentic\_guard} (\texttt{agentic\_guard/core.py}). Operating as an inline pre-trade gateway before order entry into the exchange matching engine, \texttt{agentic\_guard} intercepts every raw action generated by deep reinforcement learning, imitation learning, or LLM reasoning agents and enforces four sequential safety barriers:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Barrier 1 (Semantic Anti-Hallucination Filter):} Rejects orders with non-positive or non-finite quantities ($q \le 0$, $\text{NaN}$, $\infty$) or off-market limit prices deviating by more than $\delta_{\mathrm{dev}} = 3.0\%$ from the prevailing mid-quote, preventing prompt injection or stochastic generative collapse from corrupting the book.
    \item \textbf{Barrier 2 (Streaming Conic Feedback Certification):} Evaluates a streaming 2SLS feedback estimator in sub-25 microseconds to generate the finite-sample stability interval $[S_{\mathrm{lower}}, S_{\mathrm{upper}}]$.
    \item \textbf{Barrier 3 (Theorem~\ref{thm:m5_kreiss_solvency} Kreiss Solvency Radius Enforcement):} Dynamically clamps cumulative requested positions to $|I_t + \Delta I_t| \le r_i^* = B_i / (\mathcal{K}(J) \cdot P_t)$, bounding linear transient nonnormal inventory amplification within the clearing firm's modeled capital buffer $B_i$ under nominal Jacobian dynamics.
    \item \textbf{Barrier 4 (Three-State Fallback State Machine):} Enforces Green ($S_{\mathrm{upper}} < 0.95$, unrestricted), Yellow ($0.95 \le S_{\mathrm{upper}} < 1.0$, 50\% order throttle), and Red ($S_{\mathrm{upper}} \ge 1.0$, Mandatory Abstention). Under Red state, aggressive liquidity-taking orders (Market/IOC) are strictly halted (0\% leakage), while liquidity-providing limit orders are converted to passive pegged orders with a 75\% size reduction.
\end{enumerate}
In a benchmark 10-agent financial society simulation (\texttt{agentic\_guard/multi\_agent\_sandbox.py}) subjected to simultaneous prompt hallucinations and momentum runaway shocks, the unprotected market suffered a $-31.22\%$ flash crash with multiple bankruptcies. Under \texttt{agentic\_guard}, maximum drawdown was constrained to $-0.85\%$ (a $36.7\times$ risk reduction) with zero agent defaults and a mean interception latency of $52.81~\mu\text{s}$, well within the sub-$100~\mu\text{s}$ institutional HFT requirement. The core invariant is formally mechanized in Lean~4 (\texttt{Proofs/AgenticGuard.lean}, Theorem~\texttt{agentic\_guard\_composite\_safety}).

\paragraph{Real Level-3 order book replay and multi-provider LLM tool gateway.}
To evaluate \texttt{agentic\_guard} against live market depth rather than stylized agent interactions, we implement an institutional gateway (\texttt{agentic\_guard/institutional\_gateway.py} and \texttt{real\_lob\_benchmark.py}) that natively ingests function-calling tool payloads across OpenAI, Anthropic Claude, and Google Gemini schemas. Replaying genuine historical Level-3 depth snapshots from \texttt{BTC\_1sec.csv} ($500$ ticks, $5{,}000$ total orders) with adversarial prompt injections (fat-finger order sizes, rogue off-market quotes, and supercritical momentum cascades), the unprotected market experienced a $-350.72\%$ drawdown with $8$ out of $10$ autonomous agents defaulting ($80.0\%$ bankruptcy rate, $95\%$ Clopper--Pearson CI $[44.4\%, 97.5\%]$). In contrast, the AMIP Institutional Gateway prevented simulated agent bankruptcies in this sample ($0.0\%$ default rate in sample, $95\%$ CI $[0.0\%, 30.8\%]$) and constrained drawdown to $-16.59\%$ (a $21.1\times$ risk reduction), achieving an average pre-trade inspection latency of $3.44~\mu\text{s}$ ($99\text{th}$ percentile $9.12~\mu\text{s}$) with cryptographic SHA-256 provenance hashing structured to support pre-trade risk auditability aligned with SEC Rule 15c3-5 and MiFID II RTS 6 recordkeeping objectives.

\subsection{Executed numerical checks and empirical ledgers}
\label{sec:checks}
\subsubsection{Purpose and reproducibility}
The computations reported here test agreement between formulas and direct matrix calculations. They neither fit the model to a financial dataset nor train financial agents. \Cref{app:code} contains the exact self-contained Python/NumPy program used for the reported matrix checks. The run used NumPy 2.3.5, double-precision arrays, and seed 20260918 for the network samples. Last-digit differences across numerical libraries are immaterial to the stated tolerances.
