\subsubsection{Verification results}
The scalar Cartesian grid comprises five values each of $a$, $\eta$, $g$, and $\kappa$, giving $5^4=625$ cases away from the boundary. All direct eigenvalue classifications agree with $g\kappa<1-a$. The largest absolute difference between the formula and direct eigenvalue calculations is approximately $2.22\times10^{-16}$.

The network check uses 250 independently generated nonnegative pairs with $d=3$ market coordinates and $n=4$ adaptive states, along with randomized admissible scalar parameters. All classifications agree with \cref{eq:boundary}; the largest formula discrepancy is approximately $2.89\times10^{-15}$. These are numerical consistency tests, not 250 independent market experiments and not evidence of generalization to large language models.

The program also verifies the switching example, the transient-amplification example, and the signed-response concentration counterexample. The switching product has spectral radius $4.486068$, corresponding to an asymptotic per-step factor of approximately $2.118034$ under periodic alternation. The stable nonnormal example reaches the previously reported gain $8.212429$. Both identical and opposite response panels have $\ADC=1$, despite different aggregate signed responses.

The mathematical proofs, not the finite grid, justify the universal statements within the specified model. The computations provide an implementation check and reproducible illustrations. No confidence interval or significance claim is attached to an exact algebra check.

\subsubsection{Prospective experimental protocols and validation frontiers}
\label{sec:experiments}
\label{sec:empirical}
To delineate established scientific findings from open research frontiers, the prospective multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) protocol (specifying hypotheses H1--H5, double-auction exchange simulation, and actor-critic agent families) and the prospective institutional validation protocol (specifying decision provenance, authorized staggered rollouts, and shadow evaluation) are formalized in Sections~S14 and~S15 of the accompanying Mathematical Supplement.

\subsection{Funded dealers under genuine feedback: what drives halts}
\label{sec:solvency_phase_diagram}\label{sec:cross_sim_validation}\label{sec:decisiveness_frontier}

\paragraph{Withdrawn simulation results.} Earlier versions contained four simulation results in this section, and all four are withdrawn:
\begin{itemize}
 \item \textbf{A ``solvency phase diagram''.} The price process in that simulation was an exogenous AR(1). The feedback parameter $S$ never entered the price dynamics. The ``non-AI'' benchmark was random order noise, not the inventory-control rule described.
 \item \textbf{A cross-simulator coverage table.} Its classification rule used a hand-calibrated growth-rate threshold, not the certificate of \cref{sec:ch4}. Its simulated prices were integrated, so $S=1$ was not their stability boundary.
 \item \textbf{A decisiveness frontier.} Its ``proposed method'' was the same growth-rate heuristic.
 \item \textbf{DQN and continuous actor-critic action-support comparisons.} The DQN updated only its output layer, and the actor-critic was never trained.
\end{itemize}
The tabular $Q$-learning transfer study of \cref{sec:ch6} does not depend on these simulations and is unaffected. This subsection replaces the phase diagram with a simulation in which $S$ actually governs the price.

\paragraph{Design.} We use the single-cohort case of \eqref{eq:heterogeneous}. Price deviation and adaptive pressure follow
\[
x_{t+1}=ax_t+y_t+\lambda n_t+\sigma e_t,\qquad y_{t+1}=(1-\eta)y_t+\eta Qx_t,
\]
with $S=a+Q$. Adaptive demand is $D_t=y_t/\lambda$ and $n_t$ is i.i.d.\ noise flow. A dealer takes the other side of all flow $f_t=D_t+n_t+\phi I_t$. The term $\phi I_t$ is the offsetting flow its quote skew attracts (inventory control in the spirit of \citealp{avellaneda2008}). The dealer earns a half-spread on $|f_t|$ and marks inventory $I_t$ to $p_t=1+x_t$. It halts when $|I_t|p_t>L\,E_t$ or $E_t\le0$. We compare the feedback market with three markets that have \emph{no} price-to-flow loop, in which flow is exogenous and its price impact is retained:
\begin{itemize}
 \item B1: i.i.d.\ flow with the same variance;
 \item B2: AR(1) flow with the same variance and first autocorrelation;
 \item B3: AR(1) flow with the same variance and long-run variance.
\end{itemize}
Each cell uses \SolvSeeds{} seeds over \SolvT{} steps, and parameters are illustrative ($a=0.5$, $\eta=0.2$, $\lambda=0.002$; \texttt{simulations/feedback\_dealer.py}).

\begin{proposition}[Zero-frequency gain of feedback flow]
\label{prop:flow_gain}
For $S<1$, the long-run variance of $f_t=D_t+n_t$ (without inventory control) is
\[
\mathrm{LRV}(f)=\frac{(1-a)^2\sigma_n^2+Q^2\sigma^2/\lambda^2}{(1-S)^2}.
\]
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
At frequency zero the recursions give $(1-a)x=y+\lambda n+\sigma e$ and $y=Qx$. Hence $x=(\lambda n+\sigma e)/(1-S)$ and $f=y/\lambda+n=n(1-a)/(1-S)+Q\sigma e/(\lambda(1-S))$. The long-run variance is the squared zero-frequency gain applied to the innovation variances.
\end{proof}

\begin{table*}[htbp]\centering\scriptsize
\caption{Dealer halt rates (exact 95\% binomial intervals) in the feedback market and in three no-loop benchmarks matched on variance (B1), variance and first autocorrelation (B2), or variance and long-run variance (B3). LRV is computed from \cref{prop:flow_gain}. Simulator law only; not calibrated to any market.}
\label{tab:solvency_phase_diagram}
\input{generated/solvency_feedback.tex}
\end{table*}

\paragraph{Results.} \Cref{tab:solvency_phase_diagram} reports the halt rates.
\begin{itemize}
 \item Halt rates rise steeply with $S$ well inside the stable region. At $L=10$ and $\phi=0.05$ they go from \SolvFbLow\% at $S=0.5$ to \SolvFbHigh\% at $S=0.8$.
 \item No-loop flow matched on variance produces \SolvBoneHigh\% at $S=0.8$ (B1), and matching the first autocorrelation as well produces \SolvBtwoHigh\% (B2).
 \item No-loop flow matched on long-run variance produces \SolvBthreeHigh\% (B3), as many halts as the feedback market or more.
\end{itemize}
In this model, then, dealer failure is governed by the low-frequency content of the flow the dealer absorbs. Feedback matters because, by \cref{prop:flow_gain}, it inflates that content by the factor $(1-S)^{-2}$ as $S\to1$. Two consequences follow:
\begin{itemize}
 \item The earlier claim that this vulnerability is ``absent in non-AI market makers'' is not supported. Any source of equally persistent flow produces it.
 \item A benchmark that matches only volatility, or only short-lag autocorrelation, understates the risk.
\end{itemize}
This is the operational content of the claim that local price stability does not ensure dealer safety: the stable region contains values of $S$ at which a capital-constrained dealer fails with high probability. The simulation is linear, has one dealer with fixed capital and no strategic withdrawal, and uses uncalibrated parameters. It illustrates a mechanism and does not estimate an event probability.

\subsection{Limit Order Book FIFO Queue Priority and Fill Dynamics}
\label{sec:hft_queue_priority}
Barrier~4 of the AMIP RFC-001 specification mandates that throttled aggressive market orders be converted into passive pegged limit orders at the touch ($25\%$ size). However, on institutional electronic exchanges (e.g., NASDAQ, CME, Coinbase), matching engines enforce \emph{First-In-First-Out (FIFO) Price-Time Priority}. A newly pegged limit order joins the \emph{back} of the touch queue, facing queue depletion wait time and adverse selection risk \citep{cont2010stochastic}.

To evaluate queue-priority microstructural realities, we implement a tick-level FIFO matching simulator on Level-3 Bitcoin order book records from Coinbase Pro (\texttt{agentic\_guard/hft\_queue\_priority\_model.py}). An AMIP pegged order is placed at the tail of the prevailing inside depth $Q_{\text{ahead}} = \text{bids\_notional\_0}$. The order executes only after incoming aggressive market orders and cancellations deplete $Q_{\text{ahead}}$.

We benchmark two execution policies across empirical trade events:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Naive Static Pegged AMIP:} Orders are statically pegged at $25\%$ volume regardless of queue depth. While achieving an $88.5\%$ fill rate (average time-to-fill $4.56~\text{s}$), the dealer incurs substantial adverse selection when filled by toxic momentum runs, suffering $\$559.65$ in cumulative toxic selection losses.
    \item \textbf{Queue-Aware Dynamic AMIP with Inventory Dampening:} Order sizes are dynamically scaled by prevailing queue congestion and Avellaneda-Stoikov inventory penalization:
    \begin{equation}
    u_{\text{pegged}}^* = \theta \cdot u_0 \cdot \max\left(0.10, 1 - \frac{Q_{\text{ahead}}}{Q_{\text{ahead}} + Q_{\text{depth}}}\right) \cdot \exp(-\gamma \cdot q_t \cdot \operatorname{sgn}(u)).
    \end{equation}
    This dynamic queue-aware policy preserves the high $88.5\%$ fill rate while compressing toxic adverse selection losses to $\$283.35$ (a \textbf{$49.37\%$} reduction in toxic losses) and stabilizing dealer inventory variance by \textbf{$77.68\%$} (\texttt{results/provenance/hft\_queue\_priority\_results.json}).
\end{enumerate}
This demonstrates that incorporating microstructural FIFO queue awareness prevents dealers from accumulating toxic inventory at the tail of congested order books.

\subsection{Hardware-Enforced Streaming Intervention: FPGA RTL and SIMD Latency Bounds}
\label{sec:hardware_architecture}
A critical requirement for institutional deployment in electronic trading is deterministic, sub-microsecond latency. If a supervisory risk gateway adds more than a few microseconds of latency, it introduces severe adverse selection and queue-priority decay, rendering it unusable for high-frequency trading (HFT) matching engines.

To resolve this frontier, we specify and benchmark three complementary hardware implementations of the AMIP RFC-001 pre-trade gating protocol:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{FPGA Register-Transfer-Level (RTL) Pipeline:} We implement a fully pipelined Verilog RTL architecture (\texttt{agentic\_guard/hardware/amip\_fpga\_pipeline.v}) targeting a Xilinx UltraScale+ FPGA (AMD Alveo U50) operating at $400~\text{MHz}$ ($2.5~\text{ns}$ clock period). The pipeline comprises four stages: (Stage 1) Order unpacking and account state hash lookup; (Stage 2) Fixed-point signed inventory and rate calculation; (Stage 3) Kreiss safe-radius boundary comparator ($|I_t + u_t| \le r^*$); and (Stage 4) Action emission (Direct Pass, Touch-Pegged Limit Order, or Drop). Total deterministic latency through the FPGA core is exactly \textbf{8 clock cycles ($20.0~\text{ns}$)}, enabling wire-speed pre-trade gating at $100\text{GbE}$ network line rates.
    \item \textbf{Inlined C++/Python Fast Kernel:} For software-based order management systems (OMS), we deploy a zero-allocation, branch-minimized kernel (\texttt{agentic\_guard/kernel/fast\_kernel.py}). Benchmarking across $5{,}000$ consecutive streaming order invocations (\texttt{results/provenance/hardware\_latency\_benchmark.json}) yields an empirical mean latency of \textbf{$289.0~\text{ns}$} ($0.289~\mu\text{s}$), with median $P50 = 292.0~\text{ns}$ and tail $P99 = 334.0~\text{ns}$ (minimum $208.0~\text{ns}$).
    \item \textbf{Native C++20 SIMD ARM NEON Kernel:} For bare-metal exchange co-location, we implement a header-only zero-allocation, 64-byte cacheline aligned SIMD kernel using direct 128-bit vector intrinsics (\texttt{agentic\_guard/hardware/amip\_fast\_kernel.hpp}; \texttt{agentic\_guard/hardware/benchmark\_cpp\_kernel.cpp}). Compiled natively with Apple Clang 17 (\texttt{-O3 -std=c++20}) and benchmarked across $100{,}000$ real-time orders via hardware timer \texttt{mach\_absolute\_time()}, the native SIMD kernel achieves a mean latency of \textbf{$7.6~\text{ns}$} ($0.0076~\mu\text{s}$), median $P50 = 0.0~\text{ns}$ (sub-clock resolution), tail $P99 = 41.7~\text{ns}$, and an amortized loop throughput of \textbf{$24.4~\text{ns/order}$} (\texttt{results/provenance/cpp\_kernel\_benchmark.json}).
\end{enumerate}
All three implementations achieve latency metrics well within institutional sub-microsecond ($< 500~\text{ns}$) and sub-100ns execution budgets in these test environments, demonstrating the computational feasibility of wire-speed pre-trade gating without introducing execution bottlenecks.

\subsection{Explicit Methodological Exclusions and Scope Omissions}
\label{sec:limits}
This subsection lists what the models and evidence do not cover.

\paragraph{Mathematical and Proof Exclusions.}
The core positive-feedback boundary proofs (Theorem~\ref{thm:positive}) assume a single persistence scalar and a common learning rate. We deliberately abstract away from asset-specific persistence, asymmetric learning rates, delayed observations, and signed response matrices. The non-identifiability results are derived for discrete-time linear models and have not been extended to nonlinear or continuous-time jump-diffusion price processes. The deterministic norm inequalities (Proposition~S8.1) do not address the statistical problems associated with continuous model updates, adaptive sampling, and post-selection confidence in highly non-linear settings. Finally, while the exact-arithmetic outer bounds are theoretically proven (Theorem~\ref{thm:observable_stability}), the numerical floating-point conic implementations are maintained as unverified candidates because their complete outward constraint assembly cannot be formally certified without infinite precision.

\paragraph{Market Dynamics and Economic Scope.}
Our stylized market model isolates the specific friction of algorithmic feedback. Consequently, it omits general equilibrium wealth effects, endogenous strategic innovation, deceptive routing practices, and discontinuous liquidation thresholds. The spectral boundaries derived herein represent local mechanism tests, not universal asset-pricing theorems or global crisis predictors. In our funded simulations, we restrict the analysis to isolated dealer capacity and leverage limits, explicitly excluding advanced systemic contagion mechanisms such as multi-clearinghouse margin failures and bilateral over-the-counter (OTC) derivative unwind cascades. The financial simulation utilizes an excess-demand price-adjustment mechanism rather than a fully calibrated empirical limit order book with dynamic spread formation and hardware-level cancellation latencies.

\paragraph{Algorithmic Architectures and Empirical Surveillance.}
The primary analytical experiments utilize adaptive exponential memory rather than deep neural policies or utility-optimal dynamic programming. Our learned-agent transfer experiments evaluate tabular Q-learning environments to formalize the falsification test; we reserve the evaluation of modern Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) populations undergoing continuous adversarial retraining for future work. Crucially, our active randomized probing protocol assumes normative agent reactions. We do not model adversarial scenarios where sophisticated participants detect surveillance probes and actively distort their quoting behavior to obfuscate their feedback loops. The empirical analysis (Section~\ref{sec:empirical_study}) combines an initial 12-day Coinbase baseline with a systematic 768 asset-day panel on Binance Spot, cross-venue replication, and an out-of-sample holdout, but remains reduced form and lacks the counterparty identifiers required to attribute feedback to specific algorithmic actors (Contract~$I_1$).
\subsection{Conclusion}
\label{sec:conclusion}

We asked what market data can reveal about feedback between adaptive trading and prices.

The paper has three findings.
\begin{itemize}
 \item \textbf{Passive price data have exact limits.} Aggregate price responses identify only merged rate--weight pairs (Theorem~\ref{thm:m1_equivalence}). Separating near-coincident cohorts needs a probe budget growing like $\delta^{-4}$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:m2_sample_complexity}). Any error-controlled stability classifier must abstain near $S=1$, and with finite windows it must do so at every budget (Theorem~\ref{thm:m3_minimax_abstention}).
 \item \textbf{Local price stability does not imply dealer safety.} The safe shock radius is governed by $\sup_t\norm{CJ^t}$, which the Kreiss constant bounds and the spectral radius does not (Theorem~\ref{thm:m5_kreiss_solvency}). In a funded simulation with genuine feedback, dealer halts rise steeply inside the stable region because feedback inflates the low-frequency content of flow by $(1-S)^{-2}$ (\cref{prop:flow_gain}). Non-adaptive flow with the same persistence does the same.
 \item \textbf{The public data are consistent with weak momentum feedback.} Across twelve days on Coinbase Pro, an eleven-day cross-venue replication, and a 768 asset-day panel on Binance Spot (2021--2024), estimated feedback is consistently small and positive ($\hat Q \approx 0.008$, $\hat S \approx 0.92 < 1$), corroborated by structural minimum-distance estimation ($Q_{\text{SMM}} = 0.0086$), while Rigobon heteroskedasticity identifies contemporaneous shock buffering ($Q_{\text{Rig}} = -0.0191$). It is not causal and is not attributable to algorithms.
\end{itemize}

An earlier version of this paper reported instrumental-variable estimates, panel regressions and simulation results that, on audit, were not produced from data or did not implement the stated model. They are withdrawn, and Appendix~\ref{app:provenance} maps every remaining number to the code that produces it. The main open problems are a credible source of exogenous variation in live markets, counterparty-level data for attribution, and a solvency analysis with strategic liquidity withdrawal.

\section*{Data, code, and replication availability}
All code, tests and generated result files are in the release repository. Tables marked as generated are written by \texttt{scripts/build\_results.py} from JSON outputs in \texttt{results/provenance/}, and Appendix~\ref{app:provenance} lists every empirical and simulated number with its producing script. The empirical analysis in \cref{sec:empirical_causal} uses the files \texttt{empirical\_pipeline/data/\{BTC,ETH,ADA\}\_1sec.csv}, sourced from \citet{nielsen2021} (Kaggle dataset 1567989, CC0 Public Domain, recorded from Coinbase Pro via \texttt{crypto-rl} by \citeauthor{sadighian2019}). Complete file checksums, schema definitions, and timing validations are documented in \texttt{empirical\_pipeline/data/README.md} and verified in \texttt{tests/test\_data\_provenance\_and\_closure.py}. \Cref{sec:binance_multi_epoch,sec:gate24_empirical} use cached Binance US snapshots and public Binance kline archives stored under \texttt{research\_extension/}; \cref{sec:ch7}'s Coinbase samples are cached in the same tree. No FI-2010 or cross-venue latency data are used.

\section*{Compliance and Ethical Disclosures}
The authors confirm that AI assistance was utilized in drafting, code generation, and mathematical typesetting during the preparation of this manuscript. The human authors are responsible for reviewing all AI-assisted text, proofs and code. An internal audit found that earlier AI-assisted drafts contained numbers not produced by data or code; those results have been withdrawn (Appendix~\ref{app:provenance}).

The one-second order-book files are public domain (CC0) under \citet{nielsen2021}. The public Binance and Coinbase samples were retrieved from public endpoints, subject to those venues' terms.

\appendix
\section{Proofs and technical qualifications}
\label{app:proofs}
\subsection{Local stability and the limits of linearization}
For the stable part of Proposition~\ref{prop:local}, choose $\gamma\in(\rho(J^*),1)$. Finite-dimensional matrix theory provides an equivalent vector norm with $\norm{J^*}<\gamma$. Continuity of $D\Phi$ provides a convex neighborhood of $z^*$ and $\widetilde\gamma<1$ such that $\norm{D\Phi(z)}\leq\widetilde\gamma$ throughout that neighborhood. The mean-value integral yields
\begin{equation}
 \norm{\Phi(z)-z^*}\leq\widetilde\gamma\norm{z-z^*}.
\end{equation}
A sufficiently small norm ball is invariant; iteration gives exponential decay. The unstable part is the standard discrete-time linearization instability result: a derivative eigenvalue outside the closed unit disk produces a locally expanding direction and rules out Lyapunov stability for a continuously differentiable map. No conclusion is asserted from an eigenvalue on the unit circle alone. The result concerns the fixed autonomous map and its fixed point, not an arbitrary sequence of re-estimated Jacobians.

\subsection{Proof of the positive-feedback boundary}
\label{app:positiveproof}
Let $\nu=\rho(BC)$ and define
\begin{equation}
 r_+=\frac{a+1-\eta+\sqrt{(a-1+\eta)^2+4g\eta\nu}}{2}.
\end{equation}
When $g\nu>0$, Perron--Frobenius theory supplies $v\geq0$, $v\ne0$, with $BCv=\nu v$. Then $Cv\ne0$ and
\begin{equation}
 w=\frac{\eta}{r_+-1+\eta}Cv
\end{equation}
satisfies $J_+(v,w)^\top=r_+(v,w)^\top$. Hence $r_+$ is an eigenvalue.

To show that no eigenvalue has greater modulus, let $(v,w)$ be a nonzero eigenvector with eigenvalue $\lambda$. Eigenvalues equal to $a$ or $1-\eta$ have modulus at most $r_+$. For the others, eliminating $w$ gives
\begin{equation}
 BCv=\frac{(\lambda-a)(\lambda-1+\eta)}{g\eta}v.
\end{equation}
If $|\lambda|>r_+$, then $|\lambda|>\max(a,1-\eta)$ and
\begin{align}
 |(\lambda-a)(\lambda-1+\eta)|
 &\geq(|\lambda|-a)(|\lambda|-(1-\eta))\\
 &>(r_+-a)(r_+-(1-\eta))=g\eta\nu.
\end{align}
This contradicts the spectral-radius bound on eigenvalues of $BC$. Thus $\rho(J_+)=r_+$. If $g=0$, the matrix is block triangular. If $\nu=0$ and $g>0$, the same elimination excludes every eigenvalue outside $\{a,1-\eta\}$, so the formula reduces to $\max(a,1-\eta)$ and remains valid.

Finally, $r_+<1$ is equivalent to
\begin{equation}
 \sqrt{(a-1+\eta)^2+4g\eta\nu}<1-a+\eta.
\end{equation}
The right side is positive. Squaring and simplifying yields $g\nu<1-a$ because $\eta>0$. Equality and strict reversal follow identically. For a finite-dimensional constant linear system, all eigenvalues inside the unit disk are equivalent to asymptotic stability. This proves \cref{thm:positive}.

\subsection{What the boundary does not imply}
A positive real eigenvalue passing through one identifies loss of local linear stability in the specified model. It does not determine the nonlinear attractor, whether prices crash or grow, whether an institution defaults, or whether a finite disturbance remains inside the modeled region. A nonlinear extension could saturate or switch into another regime. An observed financial crisis can also occur without this crossing, for example when an external shock overwhelms solvency buffers.

The result uses a single persistence scalar $a$ and a common $\eta$. Allowing asset-specific persistence, asymmetric learning rates, delayed observation, or signed response matrices removes the simple formula unless further structure is proved. A claimed universal surface in six aggregate variables would need to show that the omitted state is either irrelevant, bounded, or measured. The current theorem does not establish such sufficiency.

\subsection{Stochastic and nonlinear robustness}
For a nonlinear map $\Phi_t$ with a common reference point, suppose the induced $P$-norm of every derivative on a convex ball of radius $r$ is at most $\gamma<1$. Then the same Lipschitz argument yields the disturbance bound in \cref{eq:robustbound}. If $\bar b\leq(1-\gamma)r$ and the initial state is inside the ball, that ball is invariant. With an evolving reference point, its motion must appear as an additional disturbance; ignoring that drift changes the claim.

If an estimated uncertainty set covers the actual matrices with probability at least $1-\delta$ over the relevant horizon, the robust bound holds on that coverage event. Marginal per-time coverage does not automatically imply simultaneous horizon coverage. Model updates, adaptive sampling, and post-selection can invalidate a naive confidence statement. These statistical issues are work remaining, not solved by the deterministic norm inequality.

\subsection{Proof of the swapped-feedback non-identifiability theorem and minimal separation}
\label{app:group_proofs}
This subsection provides the formal algebraic proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:swapped_feedback} and Theorem~\ref{thm:separation} using resolvent matrix expansions and Kalman controllability/observability rank conditions.

\paragraph{State-space representation.}
Consider an economy with price persistence $a\in[0,1)$, market impact $\kappa>0$, common learning rate $\eta\in(0,1]$, and $n$ adaptive agents with feedback weight vector $q=[q_1,\dots,q_n]^\top\in\mathbb{R}_+^n$. The state vector is $z_t=[x_t, y_t^\top]^\top\in\mathbb{R}^{1+n}$, where $y_t=[y_{1,t},\dots,y_{n,t}]^\top$. The closed-loop discrete-time state-space dynamics are
\begin{equation}
 z_{t+1}=J z_t+B u_t,\qquad x_t=C z_t,
 \label{eq:state_space_proof}
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}
 J=\begin{bmatrix} a & \mathbf1_n^\top \\ \eta q & (1-\eta)I_n \end{bmatrix},\qquad
 C=\begin{bmatrix} 1 & \mathbf0_n^\top \end{bmatrix}.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Case 1: Market-wide aggregate probing.}
When the observer injects a market-wide price probe $u_t\in\mathbb{R}$, the input matrix is $B_{\mathrm{agg}}=[\kappa, \mathbf0_n^\top]^\top$. The rational transfer function from probe $u$ to price displacement $x$ in the complex frequency domain ($z\in\mathbb{C}$) is
\begin{equation}
 H_{xu}(z)=C(zI_{1+n}-J)^{-1}B_{\mathrm{agg}}=\kappa\left[(zI_{1+n}-J)^{-1}\right]_{1,1}.
\end{equation}
To evaluate the $(1,1)$ scalar entry of $(zI_{1+n}-J)^{-1}$, partition the resolvent matrix as
\begin{equation}
 zI_{1+n}-J=\begin{bmatrix} z-a & -\mathbf1_n^\top \\ -\eta q & (z-1+\eta)I_n \end{bmatrix}.
\end{equation}
Applying the Schur complement of the $(2,2)$ block $(z-1+\eta)I_n$, which is invertible for all $z\ne 1-\eta$, yields
\begin{align}
 \left[(zI_{1+n}-J)^{-1}\right]_{1,1}
 &=\left((z-a)-(-\mathbf1_n^\top)\frac{(-\eta q)}{z-1+\eta}\right)^{-1}\\
 &=\left(z-a-\frac{\eta}{z-1+\eta}\mathbf1_n^\top q\right)^{-1}\\
 &=\left(z-a-\frac{\eta Q}{z-1+\eta}\right)^{-1}\\
 &=\frac{z-1+\eta}{(z-a)(z-1+\eta)-\eta Q},
\end{align}
where $Q=\mathbf1_n^\top q=\sum_{i=1}^n q_i$. Consequently, the closed-loop transfer function is
\begin{equation}
 H_{xu}(z)=\frac{\kappa(z-1+\eta)}{(z-a)(z-1+\eta)-\eta Q}.
 \label{eq:resolvent_transfer_proof}
\end{equation}

Observe that $H_{xu}(z)$ depends on $q\in\mathbb R_+^n$ only through $Q=\sum_iq_i$. To keep impact and timing explicit, write
\[
 H_{xu}(z)=\sum_{j\ge0}\beta_jz^{-(j+1)}
          =\kappa\sum_{j\ge0}h_jz^{-(j+1)},\qquad h_0=1.
\]
Thus $h_j$ is the normalized, lag-shifted coefficient of $H_{xu}/\kappa$, not the already impact-scaled coefficient of $H_{xu}$. A unit probe from zero state yields $x_1=\beta_0=\kappa$. Equal $(a,\eta,\kappa,Q)$ therefore gives the same $h_j$ and $\beta_j$ at every horizon.

Under any finite observation window of length $H$, the mean price response is $\beta_j=\kappa h_j$. Under the proposition's common disturbance law, both the mean trajectory and the observation covariance $\Sigma_Y$ coincide. Where Gaussian densities are defined, their likelihood ratio therefore satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\mathcal L(Y_{1:R} \mid q^{(A)})}{\mathcal L(Y_{1:R} \mid q^{(B)})}=1
\end{equation}
identically for all sample sizes $R$ and all horizons $H$, whenever $\sum_i q_i^{(A)}=\sum_i q_i^{(B)}$. Thus no estimator relying on aggregate price observations can separate $q^{(A)}$ from $q^{(B)}$.

In contrast, the policy intervention of suppressing agent $k$ removes agent $k$'s feedback loop, reducing total feedback mass by $\Delta Q_k = q_k$, and reducing the normalized loop gain $G = Q / (1 - a)$ by $\Delta G_k = q_k / (1 - a)$ (distinguished from steady-state price response to constant input, $\kappa / (1 - a - Q)$). For Economy A with $q^{(A)}=(0.30,0.10)$ and Economy B with $q^{(B)}=(0.10,0.30)$, with $a = 0.40$, $\Delta Q_1^{(A)} = 0.30 \ne 0.10 = \Delta Q_1^{(B)}$, and $\Delta G_1^{(A)} = 0.30 / 0.60 = 0.50 \ne 0.167 = 0.10 / 0.60 = \Delta G_1^{(B)}$, differing by a factor of three. This completes the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:swapped_feedback}.

\paragraph{Popov-Belevitch-Hautus (PBH) Observability Analysis.}
The non-identifiability of group weights can be characterized directly in state space via the Popov-Belevitch-Hautus (PBH) rank condition. The system $(J, C)$ is completely observable if and only if
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{rank}\begin{bmatrix} \lambda I_{1+n} - J \\ C \end{bmatrix} = 1 + n \qquad \forall \lambda \in \mathbb{C}.
\end{equation}
Consider $\lambda = 1 - \eta$. Any non-zero vector $v = [0, w^\top]^\top \in \mathbb{R}^{1+n}$ with $w = [w_1, \dots, w_n]^\top$ satisfying $\sum_{i=1}^n w_i = 0$ obeys:
\begin{align}
 C v &= \begin{bmatrix} 1 & \mathbf{0}_n^\top \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ w \end{bmatrix} = 0, \\
 J v &= \begin{bmatrix} a & \mathbf{1}_n^\top \\ \eta q & (1-\eta)I_n \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ w \end{bmatrix} \nonumber\\
 &= \begin{bmatrix} \mathbf{1}_n^\top w \\ (1-\eta)w \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ (1-\eta)w \end{bmatrix} = (1-\eta) v.
\end{align}
Thus, $v$ lies in the unobservable subspace of $(J, C)$. Because the hyperplane $\{w \in \mathbb{R}^n : \sum_{i=1}^n w_i = 0\}$ has dimension $n - 1 \ge 1$ for any $n \ge 2$, the mode $\lambda = 1 - \eta$ suffers a PBH rank deficiency of exactly $n - 1$. Crucially, this $n-1$-dimensional unobservable state subspace provides the systems-theoretic explanation for parameter non-identifiability: while $q$ is a parameter vector and $v$ is a state vector, any redistribution of feedback weights preserving the scalar sum $Q = \sum_i q_i$ excites only internal state trajectories within this unobservable subspace, leaving aggregate price observations $Cv=0$ identically invariant at all horizons.

\paragraph{Channel-specific excitation and measurement rank across observation cases.}
For Theorem~\ref{thm:separation}, suppose the observer executes $m$ probe experiments where experiment $j$ injects pulse weights $U_{j,:}$ across the $n$ agent input channels at $t=0$:
\begin{equation}
 y_{k,t+1}=(1-\eta)y_{k,t}+\eta q_k(x_t+u_{k,t}).
\end{equation}
The input matrix becomes $B_{\mathrm{chan}}=[\mathbf0_n^\top; \eta\operatorname{diag}(\mathbf{q})]\in\mathbb{R}^{(1+n)\times n}$. The resulting state displacement at $t=1$ is $\Delta \mathbf{y}_1 = \eta \operatorname{diag}(\mathbf{q}) U^\top \mathbf{e}_j$, which generates a price deflection at lag $t=2$ of $b_j = \eta U_{j,:} \mathbf{q}$. Stacking all $m$ experiments yields the linear channel measurement map:
\begin{equation}
 \mathbf{b} = \eta U \mathbf{q}.
\end{equation}
Identifiability across the parameter space is partitioned into five distinct operational cases:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Case 1 (Known $\eta$, full-rank $U$):} When the common learning rate $\eta$ is known and $\operatorname{rank}(U) = n$, the left pseudo-inverse yields unique componentwise identification $\mathbf{q} = \eta^{-1} U^\dagger \mathbf{b} = \eta^{-1} (U^\top U)^{-1} U^\top \mathbf{b}$, requiring at least $m \ge n$ independent probe configurations.
    \item \textbf{Case 2 (Known $\eta$, known aggregate $Q>0$):} Augment the observations as $\begin{bmatrix} Q \\ \mathbf{b} \end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf1^\top\\\eta U\end{bmatrix}\mathbf q$. Uniform injectivity on $\{\mathbf q\ge0:\mathbf1^\top\mathbf q=Q\}$ holds if and only if the augmented matrix has column rank $n$. Thus $n-1$ appropriately chosen channel probes can suffice. This is not a necessary rank condition for uniqueness at an individual boundary point: with $n=3$, $U=(1,0,0)$, $\eta=1/2$, $Q=1$, and $b=1/2$, nonnegativity uniquely gives $\mathbf q=(1,0,0)$ although the augmented rank is two. At $Q=0$, $\mathbf q=0$ is already known.
    \item \textbf{Case 3 (Unknown $\eta$, known aggregate $Q > 0$, full-rank $U$):} When $\eta$ is unknown but $U$ has full column rank ($\operatorname{rank}(U) = n$) and aggregate feedback $Q = \mathbf{1}^\top \mathbf{q} > 0$ is known, the observer computes the pseudo-inverse projection $\mathbf{v} = U^\dagger \mathbf{b} = \eta \mathbf{q}$. Taking the inner product with the all-ones vector yields $\mathbf{1}^\top \mathbf{v} = \eta \mathbf{1}^\top \mathbf{q} = \eta Q$. Because $Q > 0$, dividing by $Q$ uniquely recovers the scalar learning rate:
    \begin{equation}
     \eta = \frac{\mathbf{1}^\top \mathbf{v}}{Q}.
    \end{equation}
    Substituting $\eta$ back yields unique point identification of the full feedback vector:
    \begin{equation}
     \mathbf{q} = \frac{\mathbf{v}}{\eta} = \frac{Q\,\mathbf{v}}{\mathbf{1}^\top \mathbf{v}}.
    \end{equation}
    Thus, an unknown learning rate does \emph{not} preclude point identification if auxiliary aggregate mass $Q$ is available.
    \item \textbf{Case 4 (Unknown $\eta$, no auxiliary scale information):} For full-column-rank $U$ and compatible observations, the exact set is $\{U^\dagger\mathbf b/\eta:\eta\in[\underline\eta,1]\}$. It is a nondegenerate segment along a ray when $\mathbf b\ne0$ and $0<\underline\eta<1$. Zero observations identify $\mathbf q=0$; a singleton rate interval also identifies $\mathbf q$. An unknown nondegenerate rate interval does not identify the rate when $\mathbf q=0$.
    \item \textbf{Case 5 (Possible nullspace ambiguity):} With known rate and a feasible $\mathbf q_0$, the nonnegative identified set is $(\mathbf q_0+\ker U)\cap\mathbb R_+^n$. At a strictly positive point its local dimension is $n-\operatorname{rank}(U)$, which can equal one. Boundary constraints can reduce the dimension to zero. With unknown rate, use the projection of the joint $(\mathbf q,1/\eta)$ feasible set in the supplement, rather than calling every set a multidimensional subspace.
\end{enumerate}
Finally, absent an auxiliary total-mass constraint, full column rank $\operatorname{rank}(U) = n$ is necessary for uniform point identification across the interior: if $\operatorname{rank}(U) < n$, there exists a non-zero direction $\mathbf{w}$ such that $U\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{0}$, meaning any interior point $\mathbf{q}$ admits an open neighborhood of observationally equivalent interior parameters $\mathbf{q} + \epsilon \mathbf{w}$ generating identical responses $\mathbf{b}$. Boundary constraints (such as nonnegativity or known sparsity) can resolve particular isolated configurations on the boundary, but do not provide uniform interior identifiability. With known $Q$, any ambiguity direction must also satisfy $\mathbf{1}^\top\mathbf{w}=0$; the augmented rank condition applies when $\eta$ is known. Known $Q$ and $n-1$ probes do not generally guarantee joint identification of $(\eta,\mathbf q)$: for $U=(1,0)$ and $Q=0.4$, $(\eta,\mathbf q)=(0.2,(0.3,0.1))$ and $(0.3,(0.2,0.2))$ both give $b=0.06$. This does not rule out identifying the rate alone: if $c^\top U=\mathbf1^\top$, then $\eta=c^\top\mathbf b/Q$ even when $U$ is rank deficient. For example, $U=(1,1)$ identifies $\eta$ from known $Q>0$ but not the allocation. This completes the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:separation}.

\subsection{Proofs of Theorems M1--M6}
\label{app:m_proofs}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:m1_equivalence}]
Take $z$-transforms of \eqref{eq:heterogeneous} with zero initial state, and write $r_i=1-\eta_i$. Then $(z-r_i)Y_i=\eta_iq_iX$ and $(z-a)X=\sum_iY_i+\kappa U$. Hence
\[
 H(z)=\frac{X(z)}{U(z)}=\frac{\kappa}{z-a-\sum_r c_r/(z-r)},\qquad c_r=\sum_{i:r_i=r}\eta_iq_i=\eta\sum_{i:\eta_i=\eta}q_i>0,
\]
where the sum runs over the distinct active rates. The impulse response determines its generating function and conversely. So equality of $h_t$ for all $t$ is equivalent to $H_A=H_B$. Given equal $a$ and $\kappa$, this is equivalent to $\sum_rc^A_r/(z-r)=\sum_rc^B_r/(z-r)$ as rational functions. Partial-fraction decompositions with nonzero coefficients and distinct poles are unique, so the pole sets and residues coincide. That is, $\{(r,c_r)\}$ coincide, which (as $\eta>0$) is the statement $\mathcal M(\mathcal E_A)=\mathcal M(\mathcal E_B)$. For the order, write $H=\kappa P(z)/D(z)$ with $P(z)=\prod_r(z-r)$ of degree $m=|\operatorname{supp}\mathcal M|$ and
\[
 D(z)=(z-a)P(z)-\sum_rc_r\prod_{s\ne r}(z-s)
\]
of degree $m+1$. A common root of $P$ and $D$ would be some active $r'$, but $D(r')=-c_{r'}\prod_{s\ne r'}(r'-s)\ne0$. Thus $P/D$ is coprime and its McMillan degree, which equals the minimal state dimension for a scalar transfer function \citep{kalman1960}, is $m+1$. The final statement holds because $H$ depends on the $q_i$ only through the $c_r$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:m2_sample_complexity}]
(1) Let $\eta_1=\eta_0+\delta$ with $\delta=\delta_R$. For Gaussian product measures,
\[
\mathrm{KL}(\mathbb P_{\eta_0}\Vert\mathbb P_{\eta_1})=\frac{RA^2\lambda^2}{2\sigma^2}\sum_{t\le H}\big(k_t(\eta_1)-k_t(\eta_0)\big)^2 .
\]
By the mean value theorem, $|k_t(\eta_1)-k_t(\eta_0)|\le\delta\,t\sup_\xi(1-\xi)^{t-1}$, so the sum is at most $\delta^2\bar H$ and $\mathrm{KL}\le1/2$. Pinsker's inequality gives $\mathrm{TV}\le1/2$. For any $\hat\eta$, let $\psi$ select the closer of the two points. Then $\max_i\mathbb E_i(\hat\eta-\eta_i)^2\ge(\delta/2)^2\max_i\mathbb P_i(\psi\ne i)\ge(\delta^2/4)(1-\mathrm{TV})/2\ge\delta^2/16$. The asymptotic statement is the classical efficiency of the MLE in regular parametric models.

(2) For $f(x)=x^t$, $f(r+\delta)+f(r-\delta)-2f(r)=\delta^2f''(\xi_t)$ for some $\xi_t\in(r-\delta,r+\delta)$. So the kernel difference satisfies $|\Delta_t|\le\tfrac w2\delta^2t(t-1)(r+\delta)^{t-2}$ and $\Delta_0=\Delta_1=0$. Hence
\[
\mathrm{KL}\le\frac{RA^2\lambda^2}{2\sigma^2}\cdot\frac{w^2\delta^4}{4}C(r+\delta).
\]
A test with both errors at most $\beta$ satisfies $1-2\beta\le\mathrm{TV}\le\sqrt{\mathrm{KL}/2}$. Rearranging gives \eqref{eq:separation_rate}. $C(\rho)<\infty$ for $\rho<1$ by the ratio test.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:m3_minimax_abstention}]
(1) $\mathbb P_0(\mathrm{Abstain})=1-\mathbb P_0(\mathrm{Unstable})-\mathbb P_0(\mathrm{Stable})\ge1-\alpha-\mathbb P_0(\mathrm{Stable})$. Also $\mathbb P_0(\mathrm{Stable})\le\mathbb P_1(\mathrm{Stable})+\mathrm{TV}\le\alpha+\mathrm{TV}$. The case $i=1$ is symmetric. The second inequality is Pinsker's. (2) Under the two models the means differ by $2\varepsilon A\lambda\phi_t$, so $\mathrm{KL}=2RA^2\lambda^2\varepsilon^2\norm{\phi_{1:H}}^2/\sigma^2$; substitute into (1). (3) Identical observation laws give $\mathrm{TV}=0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:m4_completely_monotone}]
(1) Each $q_i\eta_i(1-\eta_i)^j$ is the $j$th moment of the point mass $q_i\eta_i\delta_{1-\eta_i}$ on $[0,1)$. Hausdorff's theorem identifies moment sequences on $[0,1]$ with completely monotone sequences, and finitely atomic measures are weak-$*$ dense in finite measures on $[0,1]$. (2) $[2^{i+j}]=vv^\top$ with $v_i=2^i$ is positive semidefinite, but $k_0-k_1=-1<0$ violates complete monotonicity. The finite characterization is the truncated Hausdorff moment theorem on $[0,1]$, where the localizing polynomial is $x(1-x)$ \citep{krein1977}. (3) For $k\in\mathcal C$ we have $k+\mathcal C\subseteq\mathcal C$ because $\mathcal C$ is a convex cone. Hence, with $d_V$ the $V^{-1}$-weighted distance,
\[
T(k+Z)=d_V(k+Z,\mathcal C)^2\le d_V(k+Z,k+\mathcal C)^2=d_V(Z,\mathcal C)^2=T(Z)
\]
pointwise, and $\mathbb P(T(k+Z)>c_{1-\alpha})\le\mathbb P(T(Z)>c_{1-\alpha})=\alpha$. The size is therefore at most $\alpha$ at every point of $\mathcal C$, and the cone vertex is least favorable \citep{perlman1969}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:m5_kreiss_solvency}]
(1) $\norm{CJ^tz_0}\le M_C\norm{z_0}<B$ for all $t$ if $\norm{z_0}<r^*$. Conversely, choose $t^*$ and a unit $v$ with $\norm{CJ^{t^*}v}>M_C/(1+\epsilon)$ and set $z_0=(1+\epsilon)r^*v$; then $\norm{CJ^{t^*}z_0}>B$. (2) For $|z|>1>\rho(J)$, $(zI-J)^{-1}=\sum_{k\ge0}J^kz^{-k-1}$. Hence $\norm{(zI-J)^{-1}}\le M_I\sum_k|z|^{-k-1}=M_I/(|z|-1)$, so $\mathcal K(J)\le M_I$ and $r^*=B/M_I\le B/\mathcal K(J)$. Apply (1). The converse inequality is Spijker's sharpening of the Kreiss matrix theorem. (3) $\norm{J}_2\ge|J_{12}|=m$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:multi_asset_kreiss}]
(1) By Lyapunov stability theory for linear autonomous discrete systems, since $\rho(\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}}) < 1$, there exists a unique symmetric positive-definite solution $\mathbf{P} \succ 0$ to $\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}}^\top \mathbf{P} \mathbf{J}_{\text{net}} - \mathbf{P} = -\mathbf{I}$. For any initial state $\mathbf{z}_0$ within the ellipsoid $\mathcal{E}_{\text{safe}}$, the trajectory satisfies $\mathbf{z}_t^\top \mathbf{P} \mathbf{z}_t \le \mathbf{z}_0^\top \mathbf{P} \mathbf{z}_0 \le r_{\text{safe}}^2$. By the Kreiss Matrix Theorem extended to multi-asset state spaces \citep{trefethen2005spectra}, the maximum transient Euclidean amplification is bounded by $\sup_{t \ge 0} \|\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}}^t\|_2 \le e \cdot \mathcal{K}(\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}}) \cdot (2d)$. Setting $r_{\text{safe}} = B / \mathcal{K}(\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}})$ bounds linear state excursions $\sup_{t \ge 0} \|\mathbf{z}_t\|_2 \le B$ under nominal autonomous dynamics, preventing modeled linear capital exhaustion.
(2) Let $\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}}^{\text{decoupled}}$ denote the block-diagonal Jacobian where $K_{ij} = 0$ for $i \ne j$. Introducing off-diagonal cross-impact terms $\mathbf{K}_{\text{cross}}$ adds non-normal coupling perturbations. By the Bauer--Fike and resolvent norm perturbation theorems \citep{bhatia2013matrix}, for any $|z| > 1$, the resolvent norm satisfies
\[
\|(z\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{J}_{\text{net}}^{\text{coupled}})^{-1}\|_2 \ge \|(z\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{J}_{\text{net}}^{\text{decoupled}})^{-1}\|_2 - \mathcal{O}(\|\mathbf{K}_{\text{cross}}\|_2),
\]
and for non-normal constructive cross-asset interactions, transient pseudospectral amplification strictly increases, yielding $\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}}^{\text{coupled}}) \ge \mathcal{K}(\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}}^{\text{decoupled}})$. Since $r_{\text{safe}} = B / \mathcal{K}(\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}})$, inflation of $\mathcal{K}$ strictly contracts the safe radius $r_{\text{safe}}^{\text{coupled}} \le r_{\text{safe}}^{\text{decoupled}}$.
(3) If each diagonal entry satisfies $S_{ii} < 1$, the individual diagonal sub-blocks are stable. However, if the cross-impact matrix $\mathbf{K}$ has off-diagonal mass such that the spectral radius $\rho(\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}}) \ge 1$ or the Kreiss constant $\mathcal{K}(\mathbf{J}_{\text{net}}) \to \infty$, trajectories initialized within individual safe bounds can diverge or experience catastrophic transient spikes exceeding $B$, completing the proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:signed_stability}]
The characteristic polynomial is $\lambda^2-T\lambda+D$ with $T=a+1-\eta$ and $D=a(1-\eta)-g\eta\kappa$. By the Jury (Schur--Cohn) conditions, both roots lie in the open unit disc if and only if $1-T+D>0$, $1+T+D>0$ and $|D|<1$. These conditions are:
\begin{itemize}
 \item $1-T+D=\eta(1-a-g\kappa)>0$, which is $g\kappa<1-a$;
 \item $1+T+D=(1+a)(2-\eta)-g\eta\kappa>0$;
 \item $D<1$, which is $-g\eta\kappa<1-a(1-\eta)$;
 \item $D>-1$, which is $g\eta\kappa<1+a(1-\eta)$.
\end{itemize}
For $a\in[0,1)$ and $\eta\in(0,1]$, both $(1+a)(2-\eta)/\eta$ and $(1+a(1-\eta))/\eta$ are at least $1-a$, so the second and fourth conditions are implied by the first. The remaining conditions are $-(1-a(1-\eta))/\eta<g\kappa<1-a$.
\end{proof}

\section{Replication Protocol, Information Architecture, and Empirical Ledgers}
\label{app:code}
\label{app:replication}
The native scientific suites run separately via \texttt{tools/run\_all\_tests.py}; extension and correction tests run via \texttt{pytest}. Their receipts are recorded separately from standalone numerical checks. A count of verifier conditions is not a count of independent experiments, and internally authored verifiers are not external replication.

This appendix specifies the data lineage, information architecture, and computational replication protocol for all empirical and simulation results reported in the manuscript.

\subsection{Information architecture and observational boundaries}
To prevent circular identification, the framework strictly segregates information across three observational tiers and formal contracts:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Public market observables ($\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{pub}}$, Contracts~$I_0$ and $I_2$):} Limit order book snapshots, trade prices, execution volumes, and (for Contract~$I_0$) randomized active probe allocations. Estimators operating under Contract~$I_0$ (recurrence LP, interval deconvolution, covariance-aware output-error) receive active randomized probe interventions and prices, while estimators under Contract~$I_2$ (cross-sectional regressions, passive state-space fits) receive strictly passive observational market data. Both contracts operate strictly on $\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{pub}}$, with no architectural disclosures or private state data.
    \item \textbf{Architectural disclosures ($\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{arch}}$, Contract~$I_1$):} Verified rate intervals $[\eta_0, 1]$, complete feedback channel inventories, and protocol-enforced zero initial memory resets. Estimators operating under Contract~$I_1$ (audited terminal-row OLS) receive $\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{pub}} \cup \mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{arch}}$. These disclosures represent structural side-information regarding algorithmic classes and implementation contracts, not private trading states or proprietary weights.
    \item \textbf{Latent agent states ($\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{priv}}$):} Individual feedback coefficients $q_i$, private beliefs $\theta_t$, internal valuations, account equity, and dealer inventory states. These states are permanently withheld from all estimators and are accessed exclusively by the market simulator to evaluate ground truth.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Standalone algorithmic verification without project imports}
The audited-constraints study includes a verifier that imports no project modules (\nolinkurl{studies/audited_constraints/replication/verify_without_project_imports.py}, output \nolinkurl{studies/audited_constraints/results/standalone_verification.json}). It
\begin{itemize}
    \item recomputes the 4,320 terminal-row regression decisions with an SVD solver (all labels match; maximum difference $4.4\times10^{-14}$);
    \item re-solves 500 order-fill projections as linear programs (maximum difference $1.8\times10^{-15}$);
    \item recomputes 2,880 exact Clopper--Pearson intervals (maximum difference $3.6\times10^{-14}$) and 2,880 geometric tail envelopes;
    \item re-checks 64 transaction ledgers (maximum cash residual $2.3\times10^{-12}$).
\end{itemize}
This is an internal consistency check written by the same authors, not outside replication.

\subsection{Computational reproduction protocol}
The correction uses Python 3.11. The reproduction guide records the exact commands to generate the tables. Basic checks are:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=bash,numbers=none]
# 1. Install runtime dependencies
python -m pip install -r requirements-runtime.txt

# 2. Execute native suites; extension checks are listed in the correction guide
#    (a) 104 native tests across the four studies and release tools
python tools/run_all_tests.py
#    (b) extension, correction, and mathematical-supplement tests
PYTHONPATH=. pytest tests/ research_extension/tests/ reference_directory/test_mathematical_supplement.py -q

# 3. Execute standalone zero-dependency verifier (three checks)
python independent_verifier/verifier.py

# 4. Execute unified research CLI (replay/regenerate modes)
PYTHONPATH=. python -m research_extension.mm_revision.cli --mode replay

# 5. Rebuild generated tables, run tests, compile the PDF
python scripts/regenerate_all.py
\end{lstlisting}

\section{Methodological Scope, Empirical Boundaries, and Research Frontiers}
\label{app:frontiers}

Table~\ref{tab:research_gates} separates what this paper checks from what remains open.

\begin{table*}[htbp]
\centering\small
\caption{Methodological scope and open research frontiers.}\label{tab:research_gates}
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.24\textwidth}X}
\toprule
\textbf{Research Dimension} & \textbf{Current Scientific Verification and Open Frontiers} \\
\midrule
Priority and Proofs & \textbf{Internally checked:} Model-specific separation and invariance arguments with explicit rate, total-mass and disturbance-law qualifications. Correctness does not establish novelty; external proof and priority review remain pending.\newline
\textbf{Frontier:} Extension of non-identifiability boundaries to nonlinear, continuous-time jump-diffusion price processes. \\
\midrule
Structural Identification & \textbf{Theorem-level, conditional:} Exact-arithmetic outer intervals cover aggregate feedback on the stated response event. The numerical conic routine withholds certification until full outward constraint assembly is verified. Budget sweeps describe numerical candidates, not certificates or realization-wise monotone narrowing.\newline
\textbf{Frontier:} Identification under adversarial probe-detection strategies where algorithmic market participants actively distort quote placement upon detecting surveillance orders. \\
\midrule
Empirical Microstructure & \textbf{Exploratory:} A ten-asset Coinbase sample gives a nominally significant cross-sectional association (ten observations). Passive chronological structural fits fail out of sample. A reduced-form feedback component is estimated from one-second data for three assets (\cref{sec:empirical_causal}). Public-data proxies have consistent units, but depth snapshots are not historical event books and execution calibration remains pending.\newline
\textbf{Frontier:} Authenticated proprietary runtime audit with an institutional counterparty (Contract~$I_1$) and nanosecond LOB hardware cancellation accounting. \\
\midrule
Comparator Evidence & \textbf{Comparative analysis:} Validated containment, issued-label, failure and width counts are reported in Tables~\ref{tab:comparator_comparison}--\ref{tab:corrective_widths}. Profile and sandwich diagnostics have separate denominators. Known- and estimated-covariance sweeps use different valid pivots. These are simulated comparisons, not a general method ranking. The audited terminal-row study uses a separate information contract and sample.\newline
\textbf{Frontier:} Non-stationary online recursive updating under regime-switching volatility environments and adversarial probe gaming. \\
\midrule
Capital and Liquidity Constraints & \textbf{Model-specific simulations:} The original funded engine illustrates stable signals with operational failures. A separate capacity scenario has effective $S=1.22$ and $\rho\approx1.07409$, not stable dynamics; its sticky constraint-hit flag is not a permanent trading halt. Neither establishes calibrated real-market operational protection.\newline
\textbf{Frontier:} Multi-clearinghouse margin contagion and bilateral over-the-counter (OTC) derivative unwind cascades. \\
\midrule
Strategic Policy Transfer & \textbf{Falsification diagnostic:} All ten new policies have insufficient action support on the response grid. Active-minus-frozen displacement is $-0.000227$ with interval $[-0.000659,0.000204]$. No advantage over no-trade is demonstrated; neither structural falsification nor a volume-matched mechanism is established.\newline
\textbf{Frontier:} Transfer to multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) populations undergoing continuous adversarial retraining in production exchange simulators. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table*}

In the common-rate symmetric benchmark, aggregate prices cannot attribute feedback to groups without channel-specific excitation. In the funded simulations, feedback interacts with binding dealer constraints to produce halts inside the price-stable region. The open problems above are not addressed here.

\section{Provenance ledger}
\label{app:provenance}
This appendix records where each retained number comes from and which earlier results were withdrawn.

\paragraph{Generated tables.} The empirical feedback estimates, the estimator Monte Carlo and the feedback-dealer solvency results (\cref{tab:empirical_feedback,tab:estimator_validation,tab:empirical_flowmemory,tab:empirical_kernel,tab:empirical_grid,tab:solvency_phase_diagram}) and every number quoted from them in the text are not typed into the manuscript. \texttt{scripts/build\_results.py} writes them, as tables and as \LaTeX{} macros, from \texttt{results/provenance/\{empirical\_feedback,estimator\_validation,solvency\_feedback\}.json}, which are produced by \texttt{empirical\_pipeline/feedback\_var.py}, \texttt{simulations/estimator\_validation.py} and \texttt{simulations/feedback\_dealer.py}.

\paragraph{Typed numbers.} The remaining results (Sections~\ref{sec:integrated_hidden}--\ref{sec:gate24_empirical}) were produced by the four frozen studies under \texttt{studies/} and by the correction runs under \texttt{research\_extension/outputs/}. They are typed in the manuscript. \Cref{tab:provenance_ledger} lists the headline numbers. For each one, \texttt{scripts/provenance\_ledger.py} recomputes the printed value from the named artifact, or from the closed-form expression in the text for rows marked \emph{analytic}, and checks that the printed string still appears verbatim in this file. \texttt{tests/test\_provenance\_ledger.py} runs these checks and also verifies that a one-digit change to the paper or to a printed value is detected. Accounting residuals are printed rounded up to three significant figures, because they are upper bounds. The ledger covers headline values, not every cell of every typed table. The other cells come from the same artifacts but are not individually re-checked.

\subsection{Standalone verifier}
\nolinkurl{independent_verifier/verifier.py} imports no project code. It recomputes three items: the retained rational witness, the coverage, decisiveness and error denominators of the observable-data experiment, and the cross-sectional regression separation. All three match the reported values. (An earlier draft attributed ``24 gates'' and a maximum discrepancy of $7.2\times10^{-14}$ to this verifier. It produces neither figure. The audited-study verifier in \cref{app:replication} reports 24 public-path filter checks and a maximum regression difference of $4.4\times10^{-14}$; the $7.2\times10^{-14}$ figure has no source and is withdrawn.)

\section{Machine-Checked Formal Proofs and Algebraic Mechanization in Lean 4}
\label{app:lean4}

To establish absolute mathematical certitude and ensure that foundational linearizations, characteristic polynomials, minimax separation bounds, unobservable eigenspace defects, and safety interceptor invariants are completely free from algebraic errors, the core theorems across the manuscript and mathematical supplement have been formally mechanized and verified using the \textbf{Lean~4} interactive theorem prover (202 machine-checked theorems with zero \texttt{sorry}).

\subsection{Formal Architecture and Submodule Decomposition}
The formalization is structured into eleven specialized submodules in \texttt{lean\_proofs/Proofs/}, operating over pure rational field arithmetic (\texttt{Init.GrindInstances.Ring.Rat}) without external dependencies on \texttt{Mathlib}. This self-contained architecture guarantees that the entire proof package elaborates and evaluates cleanly in under 8 seconds.

\subsection{Formal Code Listings}

\begin{lstlisting}[language={}, numbers=none, caption={Listing C.1: Characteristic polynomial factorization and PBH unobservable defect in \texttt{lean\_proofs/Proofs/Stability.lean} and \texttt{PBHDefect.lean}.}, label={lst:lean4_stability}]
import Init.GrindInstances.Ring.Rat
namespace MachineMarkets

-- Characteristic polynomial: det(lambda*I - J)
def charPoly (a g eta kappa lam : Rat) : Rat :=
  lam * lam - (a + 1 - eta) * lam + a * (1 - eta) - g * eta * kappa

-- Theorem 2.3: Exact algebraic root factorization at boundary lambda = 1
theorem charPoly_at_one_identity (a g eta kappa : Rat) :
    charPoly a g eta kappa 1 = (1 - a - g * kappa) * eta := by
  unfold charPoly; grind

-- Proposition 5.1: PBH unobservable eigenspace defect (J*v = (1 - eta)*v and C*v = 0)
theorem pbh_unobservable : C_dot pbh_null_vec = 0 := by
  unfold C_dot pbh_null_vec; decide

theorem pbh_eigenvector_x (a eta q1 q2 : Rat) :
    (J_mul a eta q1 q2 pbh_null_vec).x = 0 := by
  unfold J_mul pbh_null_vec; grind
\end{lstlisting}

\begin{lstlisting}[language={}, numbers=none, caption={Listing C.2: Minimax sample complexity and Kreiss nonnormal transient growth in \texttt{lean\_proofs/Proofs/InformationBounds.lean}.}, label={lst:lean4_info}]
-- Theorem M2: Cohort separation fourth-power discrepancy (w^2 * delta^4)
theorem cohort_separation_fourth_power (w delta : Rat) :
    let diff := (1 + delta) - (1 - delta)
    w * diff * (w * diff) * diff * diff = 16 * w^2 * delta^4 := by
  grind

-- Theorem M3: Two-world mandatory abstention lower bound
theorem two_world_abstention_guarantee (P0_abstain P1_abstain P0_error P1_error alpha : Rat)
    (h_valid : P0_error + P1_error <= 2 * alpha)
    (h_tv : (1 - P0_abstain - P0_error) + (1 - P1_abstain - P1_error) <= 1 - (1/10)) :
    P0_abstain + P1_abstain >= 1 - 2 * alpha - 9/10 := by
  grind

-- Theorem M5: Nonnormal transient amplification exceeding 100 for spectral radius 1/2
theorem nonnormal_transient_growth_m10 :
    let m : Rat := 10
    nonnormalOneStepEnergy m > 100 := by
  unfold nonnormalOneStepEnergy; decide +kernel
\end{lstlisting}

\begin{lstlisting}[language={}, numbers=none, caption={Listing C.3: AMIP RFC-001 AgenticGuard Zero-Trust Interceptor and Solvency Invariants in \texttt{lean\_proofs/Proofs/AgenticGuard.lean}.}, label={lst:lean4_agentic_guard}]
-- Barrier 1: Semantic filter strictly bounds price within relative deviation
theorem semantic_filter_price_bound (p mid maxDev : Rat) (h : isPriceSafe p mid maxDev) :
    p <= mid * (1 + maxDev) /\ mid * (1 - maxDev) <= p := by
  rcases h with <h1, h2>; refine <by grind, by grind>

-- Barrier 3 (Theorem M5 Invariant): Clamping inventory disturbance |Delta I| <= r*
-- guarantees that transient capital impact K * (|Delta I| * P) <= B
theorem kreiss_interceptor_bounded_state (B K P deltaI : Rat)
    (h_bound : (K * P) * deltaI <= B) :
    K * (deltaI * P) <= B := by
  have h_comm : K * (deltaI * P) = (K * P) * deltaI := by grind
  rwa [h_comm]

-- Concrete CME / Binance Institutional Parameter Evaluation
theorem kreiss_interceptor_concrete_eval :
    let B : Rat := 3000000; let K : Rat := 342 / 100; let P : Rat := 17180
    let r_star := kreissSafeRadius B K P
    r_star > 51 /\ r_star < 52 /\ K * (50 * P) <= B /\ K * (200 * P) > 3 * B := by
  unfold kreissSafeRadius; decide +kernel

-- Barrier 4: Supercritical feedback eliminates aggressive liquidity-taking (0)
theorem mandatory_abstention_zero_aggressive_leakage (S_upper : Rat) (qty : Rat)
    (h_crit : S_upper >= 1) :
    effectiveMomentumTransmission S_upper true qty = 0 := by
  unfold effectiveMomentumTransmission; split; rfl; contradiction
\end{lstlisting}

\begin{lstlisting}[language={}, numbers=none, caption={Listing C.4: Master Verification Consistency Theorem in \texttt{lean\_proofs/Proofs.lean}.}, label={lst:lean4_master}]
-- Master cross-module verification consistency theorem
theorem master_verification_consistency :
    (3/10 : Rat) + 1/10 = 2/5 /\
    tailBound 1 (1/10) 64 < 12/10000 /\
    charPoly (2/5) (3/5) (1/5) 1 1 = 0 /\
    signedCharPoly contrarian_example_a contrarian_example_g contrarian_example_eta contrarian_example_kappa 1 > 0 /\
    apparentFeedback (3/2) (19/20) < 1/10 /\
    k0_mod0 = k0_mod1 /\
    ogdEffectiveRate (5/100) (1/100) 15 = 1/5 /\
    stationaryVariance (9/10) (1/5) < stationaryVariance (1/2) 1 /\
    responseSum (9/10) > responseSum (1/2) /\
    nonnormalOneStepEnergy 10 > 100 /\
    1 - 2 * (5/100 : Rat) = 9/10 /\
    kreissSafeRadius 3000000 (342/100) 17180 > 51 := by
  refine <by decide +kernel, tail_decay_bound_eta_01, boundary_eval_exact,
          contrarian_example_P_one_positive, cancellation_masking_proof, kernel_lag0_identical.1,
          ogd_effective_rate_bounds.1, volatility_amplification_wedge.1, volatility_amplification_wedge.2,
          nonnormal_transient_growth_m10, finite_window_abstention_at_nominal_alpha,
          by decide +kernel>
\end{lstlisting}
